\hbar\partial_{t}U_{N}(t,\hbar)-iU_{N}(t,\hbar)\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}).

The rules for composition of kernels gives by a straight forward calculation that the kernel of the
above expression is

K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)\coloneqq\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}(\hbar\partial_{t}-i\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(y,p))e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\,dp.

By preforming
a Taylor expansion of
\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}
in the variable
y

centred at
x
we obtain that

\displaystyle\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(y,p)={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq N}\frac{(y-x)^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|=N+1}(N+1)\frac{(y-x)^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x+s(y-x),p)\,ds.

We replace
\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(y,p)
by the above Taylor
expansion in the kernel and start by considering the part of the
kernel with the first sum. Here we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}\big[\hbar\partial_{t}-i\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq N}\frac{(y-x)^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)\big]e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\big]\,dp

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}[\hbar\partial_{t}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle-i\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq N}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{\left|\alpha\right|}}{\alpha!}\partial_{p}^{\alpha}[\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x,p)e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)]]\,dp

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}(\hbar\partial_{t}-i\mathcal{P}_{N})[e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)]\,dp,

where we have used the identity
(y-x)^{\alpha}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}=(-i\hbar)^{\alpha}\partial_{p}^{\alpha}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}
, integration by parts and omitted the pre-factor
(2\pi\hbar)^{-d}
. When considering the part of the kernel
with the error term we have

\displaystyle\frac{-i}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|=N+1}(N+1)\frac{(y-x)^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x+s(y-x),p)\,dsdp

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{-i}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|=N+1}(N+1)\frac{(-i\hbar)^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}\partial_{p}^{\alpha}[e^{-it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(-it\hbar^{-1})^{j}

\displaystyle\times u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x+s(y-x),p)\,ds]dp,

where we again have used the above identity and
integration by parts. Combing the two expressions we get

\displaystyle K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}(\hbar\partial_{t}-i\mathcal{P}_{N})[e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)]\,dp

(5.15)

\displaystyle+\frac{-i}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|=N+1}(N+1)\frac{(-i\hbar)^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}\partial_{p}^{\alpha}[e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}

\displaystyle\times u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x+s(y-x),p)\,ds]dp.

In order to estimate the operator norm we will divide the kernel
into two parts. We do this by considering a part localised in
y

and the reminder. To localise in
y
we let
\psi
be a smooth
function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such
\psi(y)=1
on the set

\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,|\,\dist[y,\supp_{x}(\theta)]\leq 1\}
and supported in
the set
\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,|\,\dist[y,\supp_{x}(\theta)]\leq 2\}
. With
this function our kernel can be written as

K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)=K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)\psi(y)+K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)(1-\psi(y))

(5.16)

If we consider the part multiplied by
\psi(y)
then this part has
the form as in (
5.15
) but each term is multiplied by

\psi(y)
. By the estimate in (
5.13
) we have for the
first part of
K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)\psi(y)
the following
estimate

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}(\hbar\partial_{t}-i\mathcal{P}_{N})[e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)]\psi(y)\,dp\Big|

(5.17)

\displaystyle\leq\psi(x)\psi(y)C\hbar^{N_{0}+d}.

For the second part of
K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)\psi(y)
we have
by Leibniz’s formula and Faà di Bruno formula
(Theorem
A.1
) for each term in the sum
over
\alpha

\displaystyle\frac{(-i\hbar)^{\left|\alpha\right|}}{\alpha!}\partial_{p}^{\alpha}[e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x+s(y-x),p)\,ds]\psi(y)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{N}\frac{(-i\hbar)^{\left|\alpha\right|}}{\alpha!}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{k=0}^{N+1}(it\hbar^{-1})^{k+j}\quad\sum_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{k}\leq\alpha\\
\left|\beta_{j}\right|>0\end{subarray}}}\quad c_{\alpha,\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}}\prod_{n=1}^{k}\partial_{p}^{\beta_{n}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)

\displaystyle\times\partial_{p}^{\alpha-(\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{k})}[u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x+s(y-x),p)\,ds]\psi(y).

We note that for
j
equal
0
we have an estimate of the following
form:

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{(-i\hbar)^{\left|\alpha\right|}}{\alpha!}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{k=0}^{N+1}(it\hbar^{-1})^{k}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{k}\leq\alpha\\
\left|\beta_{j}\right|>0\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha,\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}}\prod_{n=1}^{k}\partial_{p}^{\beta_{n}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)

(5.18)

\displaystyle\times\partial_{p}^{\alpha-(\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{k})}[u_{0}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x+s(y-x),p)\,ds]\psi(y)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\sum_{k=0}^{N+1}\hbar^{N+1}\hbar^{-\frac{\delta}{2}k}\varepsilon^{-N}\psi(x)\psi(y)\leq C\hbar^{N+1}\hbar^{-\frac{\delta}{2}(N+1)}\hbar^{-N+\delta N}\psi(x)\psi(y)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\hbar^{N_{0}+d}\psi(x)\psi(y).

We note that for
j
equal
1
we have an error of the following
form:

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{(-i\hbar)^{\left|\alpha\right|}}{\alpha!}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{k=0}^{N+1}(it\hbar^{-1})^{k+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{k}\leq\alpha\\
\left|\beta_{j}\right|>0\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha,\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}}\prod_{n=1}^{k}\partial_{p}^{\beta_{n}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)

(5.19)

\displaystyle\times\partial_{p}^{\alpha-(\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{k})}[u_{1}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x+s(y-x),p)\,ds]\psi(y)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{k=0}^{N+1}\hbar^{N+1}\hbar^{-\frac{\delta}{2}(k+1)}\hbar\varepsilon^{-N}\psi(x)\psi(y)\leq C\hbar^{N+2}\hbar^{-\frac{\delta}{2}(N+2)}\hbar^{-N+\delta N}\psi(x)\psi(y)

\displaystyle\leq C\hbar^{N_{0}+d}\psi(x)\psi(y),

where we have used the estimate

|u_{1}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)|\leq c\hbar
. We note that for
j

greater than or equal to
2
, we have an error of the following form:

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{(-i\hbar)^{\left|\alpha\right|}}{\alpha!}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{k=0}^{N+1}(it\hbar^{-1})^{k+j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{k}\leq\alpha\\
\left|\beta_{j}\right|>0\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha,\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}}\prod_{n=1}^{k}\partial_{p}^{\beta_{n}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)

(5.20)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\times\partial_{p}^{\alpha-(\beta_{1}+\cdots+\beta_{k})}[u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x+s(y-x),p)\,ds]\psi(y)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq C\sum_{k=0}^{N+1}\hbar^{N+1}\hbar^{-\frac{\delta}{2}(k+j)}\hbar^{1+\delta(j-2)}\varepsilon^{-N}\psi(x)\psi(y)

\displaystyle\leq C\hbar^{N+1}\hbar^{-\frac{\delta}{2}(N+1+j)}\hbar^{1+\delta(j-2)}\hbar^{-N+\delta N}\psi(x)\psi(y)\leq C\hbar^{2+\frac{\delta}{2}(N+j-1)-2\delta}\psi(x)\psi(y)

\displaystyle\leq C\hbar^{N_{0}+d}\psi(x)\psi(y),

where we have used the estimate

|u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)|\leq c\hbar^{1+\delta(j-2)}
. Now by
combining (
5.18
), (
5.19
) and (
5.20
) we arrive at

\displaystyle\Bigl|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|=N+1}(N+1)\frac{(-i\hbar)^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}\partial_{p}^{\alpha}[e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}

\displaystyle\times u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{N}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(x+s(y-x),p)\,ds]\psi(y)\,dp\Bigr|\leq C\hbar^{N_{0}+d}\psi(x)\psi(y).

Combining this estimate with (
5.17
) we have

|K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)\psi(y)|\leq C\hbar^{N_{0}}\psi(x)\psi(y),

(5.21)

Now we turn
to the term
K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)(1-\psi(y))
. On the support
of this kernel we have

1\leq\left|x-y\right|

due to the definition of
\psi
. This imply we can divide by the
difference between
x
and
y
where the kernel is supported. The
idea is now to multiply the kernel with

\frac{\left|x-y\right|}{\left|x-y\right|}
to an appropriate power
\eta
. We
take
\eta
such

\frac{m(x+s(y-x),p)}{\left|x-y\right|^{2\eta}}\leq\frac{C}{\left|x-y\right|^{d+1}}\quad\text{for }(x,p)\in\supp(\theta),

where
m
is the tempered weight function associated to our operator. The
existence of such a
\eta
is ensured by the definition of the tempered
weight. By (
5.15
) the kernel

K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)(1-\psi(y))
is of the form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}\varphi(x,y,p;\hbar,\varepsilon)(1-\psi(y))dp,

where the exact form of
\varphi
is not
important at the moment. Now for our choice of
\eta
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}\varphi(x,y,p;\hbar,\varepsilon)(1-\psi(y))dp

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}\frac{\left|x-y\right|^{2\eta}}{\left|x-y\right|^{2\eta}}\varphi(x,y,p;\hbar,\varepsilon)(1-\psi(y))dp

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(-i\hbar)^{2\eta}\sum_{\left|\gamma\right|=\eta}c_{\gamma}\partial_{p}^{2\gamma}(e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle})\frac{1}{\left|x-y\right|^{2\eta}}\varphi(x,y,p;\hbar,\varepsilon)(1-\psi(y))dp

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}\frac{1-\psi(y)}{\left|x-y\right|^{2\eta}}\sum_{\left|\gamma\right|=\eta}c_{\gamma}(i\hbar)^{2\eta}\partial_{p}^{2\gamma}\varphi(x,y,p;\hbar,\varepsilon)dp.

By analogous estimates to the estimate used above we have

\Big|\frac{1-\psi(y)}{\left|x-y\right|^{2\eta}}\sum_{\left|\gamma\right|=\eta}(i\hbar)^{2\eta}\partial_{p}^{2\gamma}\varphi(x,y,p;\hbar,\varepsilon)\Big|\leq C\hbar^{2\eta(1-\frac{\delta}{2})+1+\frac{\delta}{2}(N-1)}\frac{1-\psi(y)}{\left|x-y\right|^{d+1}}\bm{1}_{\supp(\theta)}(x,p),

where the term
\hbar^{-\eta\delta}
is due to the exponentials
e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}
in
\varphi
which gives
it\hbar^{-1}
when we take a derivative with respect to
p_{j}
for all
j
in
\{1,\dots,d\}
and that
|t|\leq\hbar^{1-\frac{\delta}{2}}
. The rest of the powers in
\hbar
is found analogous to above. Hence we have

\displaystyle|K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)(1-\psi(y))|\leq{}

\displaystyle C\hbar^{2\eta(1-\frac{\delta}{2})+1+\frac{\delta}{2}(N-1)-d}\bm{1}_{\supp_{x}(\theta)}(x)\frac{1-\psi(y)}{\left|x-y\right|^{d+1}}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C\hbar^{N_{0}}\psi(x)\frac{1-\psi(y)}{\left|x-y\right|^{d+1}}.

By combining this with (
5.16
) and
(
5.21
) we have

|K(x,y;\varepsilon,\hbar)|\leq C\hbar^{N_{0}}\Big[\psi(x)\psi(y)+\bm{1}_{\supp_{x}(\theta)}(x)\frac{1-\psi(y)}{\left|x-y\right|^{d+1}}\Big].

(5.22)

We have by definition of
\psi
the estimates

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\psi(x)\psi(y)+\bm{1}_{\supp_{x}(\theta)}(x)\frac{1-\psi(y)}{\left|x-y\right|^{d+1}}|\,dy\leq c+\int_{\left|y\right|\geq 1}\frac{1}{\left|y\right|^{d+1}}\,dy\leq C_{1}

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\psi(x)\psi(y)+\bm{1}_{\supp_{x}(\theta)}(x)\frac{1-\psi(y)}{\left|x-y\right|^{d+1}}|\,dx\leq C_{2}

These estimates combined with the Schur test,
(
5.14
) and (
5.22
) gives

\lVert\hbar\partial_{t}U_{N}(t,\hbar)-iU_{N}(t,\hbar)A_{\varepsilon}\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leq C\hbar^{N_{0}}

for
\left|t\right|\leq\hbar^{1-\frac{\delta}{2}}
. This is the desired
estimate which ends the proof.
∎

In the previous proof we constructed a microlocal approximation for
the propagator for short times dependent on
\hbar
. It would be
preferable to not have this dependence of
\hbar
in the time. In the
following Lemma we prove that under a non-critical condition on the
principal symbol a localised trace of the approximation becomes
negligible.

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
be a
\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible
operator of regularity
\tau\geq 1
with tempered weight
m
which is
self-adjoint for all
\hbar
in
(0,\hbar_{0}]
and with

\varepsilon\geq\hbar^{1-\delta}
for a
\delta\in(0,1)
. Let

\theta(x,p)
be a function in

C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{p})
. Suppose

\left|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\right|\geq c>0\quad\text{for all }(x,p)\in\supp(\theta),

where
a_{\varepsilon,0}
is the principal symbol of

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. Moreover let the operator
U_{N}(t,\hbar)
be
the one constructed in Theorem
5.1

with the function
\theta
. Then for

\left|t\right|\in[\frac{1}{2}\hbar^{1-\frac{\delta}{2}},1]
and every

N_{0}
in
\mathbb{N}
it holds

\left|\Tr[U_{N}(t,\hbar)\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(\theta)]\right|\leq C\hbar^{N_{0}}

for a constant
C>0
, which depends on the constant from the
non-critical condition.

Proof.

Recall that the kernel of
U_{N}(t,\hbar)
is given by

K_{U_{N}}(x,y,t,\varepsilon,\hbar)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}u_{N}(x,p,t,\hbar,\varepsilon)\,dp,

where

u_{N}(x,p,t,\hbar,\varepsilon)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon).

From Theorem
5.1
we have the
estimate

\sup_{x,p}\sup_{\left|t\right|\leq 1}|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}\partial_{p}^{\beta}u_{N}(x,p,t,\hbar,\varepsilon)|\leq C_{\alpha\beta}\hbar^{(\delta-1)N}\varepsilon^{-\left|\alpha\right|}.

(5.23)

This initial estimate is a priori not desirable as it implies the trace is of order
\hbar^{(\delta-1)N-d}
. Due to the form of the kernels for the operators
U_{N}(t,\hbar)
and
\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(\theta)
it immediate follows that the kernel for the composition is given by

(x,y)\mapsto\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}u_{N}(x,p,t,\hbar,\varepsilon)\theta(y,p)\,dp.

With this expression for the kernel we get that the trace is given by

\Tr[U_{N}(t,\hbar)\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(\theta)]=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}u_{N}(x,p,t,\hbar,\varepsilon)\theta(x,p)\,dxdp

Since we suppose
\left|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}\right|\geq c>0
on
the support of
\theta(x,p)
we have

\displaystyle\Tr[U_{N}(t,\hbar)\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(\theta)]

(5.24)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\partial_{p_{j}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))^{2}}{\left|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\right|^{2}}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}u_{N}(x,p,t,\hbar,\varepsilon)\theta(x,p)\,dxdp

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{-i\hbar t^{-1}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\partial_{p_{j}}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon}(x,p)}\frac{\partial_{p_{j}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}{\left|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\right|^{2}}u_{N}(x,p,t,\hbar,\varepsilon)\theta(x,p)\,dxdp

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\frac{i\hbar t^{-1}}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\partial_{p_{j}}\Big[\frac{\partial_{p_{j}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}{\left|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\right|^{2}}u_{N}(x,p,t,\hbar,\varepsilon)\theta(x,p)\Big]\,dxdp.

Combining (
5.23
), (
5.24
) and our assumptions on
t
we see that we have gained
\hbar^{\frac{\delta}{2}}
compared to our naive first estimate. To obtain the desired estimate we iterate the argument in (
5.24
) until an error of the desired order has been obtained. This concludes the proof.
∎

The previous Lemma showed that under a non-critical assumption on the
principal symbol a localised trace of our approximation becomes
negligible. But we would also need a result similar to this for the
true propagator. Actually this can be proven in a setting for which we
will need it, which is the content of the next Thoerem. An
observation of this type was first made by Ivrii (see

[
13
]
). Here we will follow the proof of such a statement
as made by Dimassi and Sjöstrand in
[
6
]
. The
statement is:

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
be a
\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible
operator of regularity
\tau\geq 1
which satisfies
Assumption
4.1
, has a bounded principal
symbol and suppose there exists a
\delta
in
(0,1)
such that

\varepsilon\geq\hbar^{1-\delta}
. Furthermore, suppose there exists a number

\eta>0
such
a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}([-2\eta,2\eta])
is compact
and a constant
c>0
such

\left|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\right|\geq c\quad\text{for all }(x,p)\in a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}([-2\eta,2\eta]),

where
a_{\varepsilon,0}
is the principal symbol of

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. Let
f
be in
C_{0}^{\infty}((-\eta,\eta))
and

\theta
be in
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{p})
such that

\supp(\theta)\subset a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}((-\eta,\eta))
.

Then there
exists a constant
T_{0}>0
such that if
\chi
is in

C_{0}^{\infty}((\frac{1}{2}\hbar^{1-\gamma},T_{0}))
for a
\gamma
in

(0,\delta]
, then for every
N
in
\mathbb{N}
, we have

\left|\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta)]\right|\leq C_{N}\hbar^{N}

uniformly for
s
in
(-\eta,\eta)
.

Remark 5.5
.

Theorems of this type for non-regular operators can be found in the works of Ivrii see
[
17
]
and Zielinski see
[
30
,
32
]
. In both cases the proof of such theorems is different from the one we present here. The techniques used by both is based on propagation of singularities. The propagation of singularities is not directly present in the proof presented here but hidden in the techniques used.

In both
[
6
]
and
[
17
]
they assume the symbol to microhyperbolic in some direction. It might also be possible to extend the Theorem here to a general microhyperbolic assumption instead of the non-critical assumption. The challenge in this will be that for the proof to work under a general microhyperbolic assumption the symbol should be change such that microhyperbolic assumption similar to the non-critical assumption is achieved. This change might be problematic to do since it could mix the rough and non rough variables.

The localising operators
\OpW(\theta)
could be omitted if the first step of the proof is change to introducing them by applying Lemma
4.14
. We have chosen to state the theorem with them since when we will apply the theorem we have the localisations.

Proof.

We start by remarking that it suffices to show the estimate with a
function
\chi_{\xi}(t)=\chi(\frac{t}{\xi})
, where
\chi
is in

C_{0}^{\infty}((\frac{1}{2},1))
uniformly for
\xi
in

[\hbar^{1-\gamma},T_{0}]
. Indeed assume such an estimate has been
prove. We can split the interval
(\frac{1}{2}\hbar^{1-\gamma},T_{0})
in

\frac{2T_{0}}{\hbar^{1-\gamma}}
intervals of size

\frac{1}{2}\hbar^{1-\gamma}
and make a partition of unity which
members is supported in each of these intervals. By linearity
of the inverse Fourier transform and trace we would have

\displaystyle|\Tr[\OpW

\displaystyle(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta)]|

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{M(\hbar)}\left|\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi_{j}}](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta)]\right|\leq\tilde{C}_{N}\hbar^{N-1+\delta}.

Hence we will consider the trace

\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta)],

with
\chi_{\xi}(t)=\chi(\frac{t}{\xi})
, where
\chi
is in

C_{0}^{\infty}((\frac{1}{2},1))
and
\xi
in
[\hbar^{1-\gamma},T_{0}]
. For
the rest of the proof we let a
N
in
\mathbb{N}
be given as the
error we desire.

Without loss of generality we can assume
\theta=\sum_{k}\theta_{k}
,
where the
\theta_{k}
’s satisfies that if

\supp(\theta_{k})\cap\supp(\theta_{l})\neq\emptyset
then there exists

j
in
\{1,\dots,d\}
such

|\partial_{p_{j}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)|>\tilde{c}
on the set

\supp(\theta_{k})\cup\supp(\theta_{l})
. With this splitting of

\theta
we have

\displaystyle\Tr[\OpW(\theta)

\displaystyle f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta)]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k}\sum_{l}\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta_{l})].

By the cyclicity of the trace and the formulas for composition of
pseudo-differential operators we observe if

\supp(\theta_{k})\cap\supp(\theta_{l})=\emptyset
then the term is
negligible. Hence what remains is the terms with

\supp(\theta_{k})\cap\supp(\theta_{l})\neq\emptyset
. All terms of the
form are estimated with analogous techniques but some different
indexes. Hence we will suppose we have a pair
\theta_{k}
and
\theta_{l}
of sets such that

\supp(\theta_{k})\cap\supp(\theta_{l})\neq\emptyset
and

|\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)|>\tilde{c}
on the set

\supp(\theta_{k})\cup\supp(\theta_{l})
. This imply we either have

\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)>\tilde{c}
or

-\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)>\tilde{c}
. We suppose we
are in the first case. The other case is treated in the same manner
but with a change of some signs.

To sum up we have reduced to the case where we consider

\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta_{l})],

with
\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)>\tilde{c}
on the the
set
\supp(\theta_{k})\cup\supp(\theta_{l})
. The next step is to change
the principal symbol of our operator such it becomes global
microhyperbolic in the direction
(\bm{0};(1,0,\dots,0))
, where
\bm{0}
is the
d
-dimensional vector with only zeros.

We let
\varphi_{2}
be a function in
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{x}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{p}^{d})

such
\varphi_{2}(x,p)=1
on a small neighbourhood of

\supp(\theta_{k})\cup\supp(\theta_{l})
and have support contained in
the set

\big\{(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\,|\,|\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)|>\tfrac{\tilde{c}}{2}\big\}.

Moreover we let

\varphi_{1}
be a function in
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R}_{x}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{p}^{d})
such

\varphi_{1}(x,p)=1
on
\supp(\varphi_{2})
and such that

\supp(\varphi_{1})\subseteq\big\{(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\,|\,|\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)|>\tfrac{\tilde{c}}{4}\big\}.

(5.25)

With these functions we define the symbol

\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)=a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\varphi_{1}(x,p)+C(1-\varphi_{2}(x,p)),

where the constant
C
is chosen such

\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\geq 1+\eta
outside the support
of
\varphi_{2}(x,p)
. We have

\partial_{p_{1}}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)=(\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0})(x,p)\varphi_{1}(x,p)+a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)(\partial_{p_{1}}\varphi_{1})(x,p)-C\partial_{p_{1}}\varphi_{2}(x,p).

Hence there exist constants
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
such

\partial_{p_{1}}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\geq c_{0}-c_{1}(\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))^{2},

(5.26)

for all
(x,p)
in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. To see this we observe that on

\supp(\theta_{k})\cup\supp(\theta_{l})
we have the inequality

\partial_{p_{1}}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\geq\tilde{c}.

By continuity there exists an open neighbourhood
\Omega
of

\supp(\theta_{k})\cup\supp(\theta_{l})
such

\partial_{p_{1}}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\geq\frac{\tilde{c}}{3}
and
(1-\varphi_{2})\neq 0
on
\Omega^{c}
. Hence
outside
\Omega
we get the the bound

\partial_{p_{1}}\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\geq c_{0}-c_{1}(\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))^{2},

by choosing
c_{1}
sufficiently large.
This estimates is that our new symbol is global microhyperbolic in
the direction
(\bm{0};(1,0,\dots,0))
.

Our assumptions on the operator
{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
imply the
form

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)=\sum_{j=0}^{N_{0}}\hbar^{j}\OpW(a_{\varepsilon,j})+\hbar^{N_{0}+1}R_{N_{0}}(\hbar,\varepsilon),

where
N_{0}
is chosen such

\hbar^{N_{0}+1}\lVert R_{N_{0}}(\hbar,\varepsilon)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leq C\hbar^{N+d}.

By
\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
we denote the operator obtained by
taking the
N_{0}
first terms of
A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
and
exchanging the principal symbol
a_{\varepsilon,0}
of

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
by
\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}
. Note that
the operator
\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
still satisfies
Assumption
4.1
as the original symbols where
assumed to be bounded. We have

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)=\OpW(a_{\varepsilon,0}-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0})+\hbar^{N_{0}+1}R_{N_{0}}(\hbar,\varepsilon),

and by construction is

a_{\varepsilon,0}-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}
supported away from

\supp(\theta_{k})\cup\supp(\theta_{l})
. Hence if we apply the resolvent identity

(z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}-(z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}=(z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))(z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1},

and use the formula for composition of operators we get for

N_{1}\geq\tau
the estimate

\displaystyle\lVert\OpW(\theta_{k})

\displaystyle((z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}-(z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1})\OpW(\theta_{l})\rVert_{\Tr}

(5.27)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle C\hbar^{-d}\lVert\OpW(\theta_{k})[(z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))(z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}]\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle C_{N_{1}}\frac{\hbar^{(N_{1}-\tau)\delta+\tau-d}}{\left|\im(z)\right|^{N+2}}+C_{N_{0}}\frac{\hbar^{N}}{\left|\im(z)\right|^{2}},

for
z
in
\mathbb{C}
with
|\im(z)|>0
. In order to use the above estimate we will use
Theorem
4.10
and hence we need to make an
almost analytic extensions of the function
f
. Let
\tilde{f}
be
an almost analytic extension of
f
, such
\tilde{f}
is in

C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{C})
and

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(x)

\displaystyle=f(x)\text{ for all }x\in\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}\tilde{f}(z)

\displaystyle\leq C_{N}\left|\im(z)\right|^{N}\text{ for all
}N\in\mathbb{N}.

Such an extension exists according to
Remark
4.8
. As

\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)
is an analytic function in

z
we have by Theorem
4.10
the identity

\displaystyle\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta_{l})]

(5.28)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})(z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}\OpW(\theta_{l})]\,L(dz).

This identity is also true for
\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. On
the support of
\tilde{f}
we have

\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)=\frac{1}{2\pi\hbar}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{it\hbar^{-1}(s-z)}\chi_{\xi}(t)\,dt\leq C\frac{\xi}{\hbar}.

Now by the properties of
\tilde{f}
, (
5.27
),
(
5.28
) and the above estimate we have for
N_{1}\geq\tau

\displaystyle|\Tr

\displaystyle[\OpW(\theta_{k})f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta_{l})]

\displaystyle-\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})f(\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta_{l})]|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}|\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\times\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})((z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}-(z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1})\OpW(\theta_{l})]|\,L(dz)

\displaystyle\leq C_{N_{1}}\hbar^{(N_{1}-\tau)\delta+\tau-d-1}+C\hbar^{N}.

Hence by choosing
N_{1}
sufficiently large we can change the
principal symbol. Note that the constant
C_{N_{1}}
also depends on
the symbols.

For the reminder of the proof we will omit the tilde on the operator
and principal symbol but instead assume the principal symbol to be
global micro-hyperbolic in the direction
(\bm{0};(1,0,\dots,0))

((
5.26
) without the tildes).

In order to estimate

\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta_{l})],

we will need an auxiliary function. Let
\psi
be in
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})

such
\psi(t)=1
for
t\leq 1
and
\psi(t)=0
for
t\geq 2
. Moreover
let
M
be a sufficiently large constant which will be fixed later
and put

\psi_{\mu_{1}}(z)=\psi\Big(\frac{\im(z)}{\mu_{1}}\Big),

where
\mu_{1}=\frac{M\hbar}{\xi}\log(\frac{1}{\hbar})
. With this
function we have

|\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}})|\leq\begin{cases}C_{N}\left|\im(z)\right|^{N},&\text{if }\im(z)<0\\
C_{N}\psi_{\mu_{1}}(z)\left|\im(z)\right|^{N}+\mu_{1}^{-1}\bm{1}_{[1,2]}(\frac{\im(z)}{\mu_{1}}),&\text{if }\im(z)\geq 0,\end{cases}

(5.29)

for any
N
in
\mathbb{N}
. Again we can use
Theorem
4.10
for the operator

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
on the function

(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)
. This gives

\displaystyle(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}})(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)(z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}\,L(dz),

where we have used that
\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)
is
an analytic function in
z
. Hence the trace we consider is

\displaystyle\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta_{l})]

(5.30)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})(z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}\OpW(\theta_{l})]\,L(dz)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\im(z)<0}\cdots\,L(dz)-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\im(z)\geq 0}\cdots\,L(dz)

If we shortly investigate each of the integrals. Firstly we note the
bound

\left|\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})(z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}\OpW(\theta_{l})]\right|\leq\frac{C}{\hbar^{d}\left|\im(z)\right|}.

If we consider the integral over the negative imaginary part we have

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\im(z)<0}

\displaystyle\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})(z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}\OpW(\theta_{l})]\,L(dz)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2N}\xi}{\pi\hbar^{d+1}}\int_{\im(z)<0}\frac{\im(z)^{2N}}{|\im(z)|}e^{\frac{\xi\im(z)}{2\hbar}}\,d\im(z)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2N}}{\pi\hbar^{d}}(\frac{\hbar}{\xi})^{2N-2}\leq\tilde{C}\hbar^{(2N-2)\gamma-d},

for any
N
in
\mathbb{N}
. We have in the above calculation used
integration by parts and the estimate

\left|\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\right|\leq C\frac{\xi}{\hbar}e^{\frac{\xi\im(z)}{2\hbar}}.

The above estimate imply that the contribution to the trace from the
negative integral is negligible. If we split the integral over
positive imaginary part up according to
\mu_{1}
we have by
(
5.29
) the estimate

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{1}{\pi}

\displaystyle\int_{0\leq\im(z)\leq\mu_{1}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})(z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}\OpW(\theta_{l})]\,L(dz)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{2N}\xi}{\pi\hbar^{d+1}}\int_{0\leq\im(z)\leq\mu_{1}}\psi_{\mu_{1}}(z)\left|\im(z)\right|^{N}e^{\frac{\xi\im(z)}{2\hbar}}\,d\im(z)

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{C}\frac{\xi}{\hbar^{d+1}}\mu_{1}^{N+1}\leq\tilde{C}\frac{\xi}{\hbar^{d+1}}\frac{M\hbar}{\xi}\log(\frac{1}{\hbar})^{N+1}\leq\tilde{C}M\hbar^{(N+1)\gamma-d-1},

for any
N
in
\mathbb{N}
. Hence this terms also becomes
negligible. What remains from (
5.30
) is the expression

-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\im(z)>\mu_{1}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})(z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}\OpW(\theta_{l})]\,L(dz).

(5.31)

In order to estimates this we will need to change all our
operators. This is done by introducing an auxiliary variable in the
symbols and make an almost analytic extension in this
variable. Recall we have change the operator

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
such it is a sum of Weyl quantised pseudo-differential operators. In the following we let
q(x,p)
be
one of our symbols and we let

q_{t}(x,p)=q(x,(p_{1}+t,p_{2},\dots,p_{d}))
. We now take
t
be complex
and make an almost analytic extension
\tilde{q}_{t}
of
q_{t}
in t
according to Definition
4.7
for

|\im(t)|<1
. The form of
\tilde{q}_{t}
is

\tilde{q}_{t}(x,p)=\sum_{r=0}^{n}(\partial_{p_{1}}^{r}q)(x,(p_{1}+\re(t),p_{2},\dots,p_{d}))\frac{(i\im(t))^{r}}{r!},

Recalling the identity

\OpW(q_{\re(t)})=e^{-i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}\OpW(q)e^{i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}},

we have

\OpW(\tilde{q}_{t})=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\frac{(i\im(t))^{r}}{r!}e^{-i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}\OpW(\partial_{p_{1}}^{r}q)e^{i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}.

(5.32)

If we take derivatives with respect to
\re(t)
and
\im(t)
in
operator sense we see

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\re(t)}\OpW(\tilde{q}_{t})

\displaystyle=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\sum_{r=0}^{n}\frac{(i\im(t))^{r}}{r!}e^{-i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}[x_{1},\OpW(\partial_{p_{1}}^{r}q)]e^{i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{n}\frac{(i\im(t))^{r}}{r!}e^{-i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}\OpW(\partial_{p_{1}}^{r+1}q)e^{i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\im(t)}\OpW(\tilde{q}_{t})

\displaystyle=i\sum_{r=1}^{n}\frac{(i\im(t))^{r-1}}{(r-1)!}e^{-i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}\OpW(\partial_{p_{1}}^{r}q)e^{i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}

\displaystyle=i\sum_{r=0}^{n-1}\frac{(i\im(t))^{r}}{r!}e^{-i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}\OpW(\partial_{p_{1}}^{r+1}q)e^{i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}.

In the above calculation the unbounded operator
x_{1}
appear, but for all the
symbols we consider the commutator
[x_{1},\OpW(\partial_{p_{1}}^{r}q)]

will be bounded. This calculation gives

\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\re(t)}+i\frac{\partial}{\partial\im(t)}\Big)\OpW(\tilde{q}_{t})=\frac{(i\im(t))^{n}}{n!}e^{-i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}\OpW(\partial_{p_{1}}^{n+1}q)e^{i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}

This imply

\displaystyle\big\lVert\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{t}}\OpW(\tilde{\theta}_{j,t})\big\rVert_{\Tr}\leq C_{n}\hbar^{-d}\left|\im(t)\right|^{n}\quad\quad\text{for $j=k,l$}

(5.33)

\displaystyle\big\lVert\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{t}}\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;t)\big\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leq C_{n}\left|\im(t)\right|^{n},

for any
n
in
\mathbb{N}
by choosing an almost analytic expansion of this
order. The operator
\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;t)
is the operator
where we have made the above construction for each symbol in the
expansion of the operator. Moreover we have by the construction of

\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;t)

\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;t)=e^{-i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)e^{i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}+i\im(t)B_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;t)

where
B_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;t)
is a bounded operator this form is obtained from (
5.32
) with
q
replaced by the symbol of
A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. This gives

z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;t)=(z-U^{*}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)U)[I+(z-U^{*}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)U)^{-1}i\im(t)B_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;t)],

where
U=e^{i\re(t)\hbar^{-1}x_{1}}
. Hence if

\left|\im(t)\right|\leq\tfrac{\left|\im(z)\right|}{C_{1}}
the operator

z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;t)
has an inverse where

C_{1}\geq\lVert B_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;t)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}+1
.
This imply that the following function

\eta(t,z)=\Tr[\OpW(\tilde{\theta}_{k,t})(z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;t))^{-1}\OpW(\tilde{\theta}_{l,t})]

is well defined for
\left|\im(t)\right|\leq\tfrac{\left|\im(z)\right|}{C_{1}}
. The function have by construction the properties

\displaystyle\left|\eta(t,z)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c}{\hbar^{d}\left|\im(z)\right|}

\displaystyle|\Big(\frac{\partial}{\partial\re(t)}+i\frac{\partial}{\partial\im(t)}\Big)\eta(t,z)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{n}\left|\im(t)\right|^{n}}{\hbar^{d}\left|\im(z)\right|^{2}}.

for
n
in
\mathbb{N}
. But by cyclicity of the trace the function

\eta(t,z)
is independent of
\re(t)
. Hence we have

\left|\eta(\pm i\im(t),z)-\eta(0,z)\right|\leq\frac{c_{N}\left|\im(t)\right|^{n}}{\hbar^{d}\left|\im(z)\right|^{2}}

by the fundamental theorem of calculus. The construction of
\eta

gives us that

\eta(0,z)=\Tr[\OpW(\theta_{k})(z-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{-1}\OpW(\theta_{l})].

Hence we can exchange the trace in (
5.31
) by

\eta(-i\frac{\mu_{1}}{C_{1}},z)
with an error of the order

\hbar^{\gamma n-d}
. This is due to our choice of

\mu_{1}=\frac{M\hbar}{\xi}\log(\frac{1}{\hbar})
in the start of the
proof and that the integral is only over a compact region where

\left|\im(z)\right|>\frac{\mu_{1}}{C_{1}}
due to the definition of
\psi_{\mu_{1}}
. It now remains to estimate the
term

-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\im(z)>\mu_{1}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\eta(-i\mu_{2},z)\,L(dz),

(5.34)

where

\eta(-i\mu_{2},z)=\Tr[\OpW(\tilde{\theta}_{k,-i\mu_{2}})(z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;-i\mu_{2}))^{-1}\OpW(\tilde{\theta}_{l,-i\mu_{2}})],

and
\mu_{2}=\frac{\mu_{1}}{C_{1}}
. From the construction of the almost
analytic extension we have the following form of the principal
symbol of
z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;-i\mu_{2})

z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p;-i\mu_{2})=z-(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)-i\mu_{2}(\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0})(x,p)+\mathcal{O}(\mu_{2}^{2})).

For
-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4}<\im(z)<0
, where
c_{0}
is the constant
from the global micro-hyperbolicity (
5.26
). We have by
the global micro-hyperbolicity for
\left|\re(z)\right|<\eta
and
\hbar

sufficiently small

\im(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p;-i\mu_{2}))\geq c_{0}\mu_{2}+\im(z)-C\mu_{2}(\re(z)-a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))^{2}.

To see this recall how the principal symbol was changed and that if

\re(z)-a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)
is zero or small then is

(\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0})(x,p)>2c_{0}
hence we have to
assume
\hbar
sufficiently small. This implies there exists a
C_{2}

such we have the inequality

\displaystyle\im(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p;-i\mu_{2})

\displaystyle+C_{2}\mu_{2}(\overline{z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p;-i\mu_{2})})(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p;-i\mu_{2}))

\displaystyle\geq\frac{c_{0}}{2}\mu_{2}+\im(z),

Where we again assume
\hbar
sufficiently small and that all terms
from the product in the above equation which is not

(\re(z)-a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))^{2}
comes with at least one extra

\mu_{2}
. Now by Theorem
3.28
we have for every
g
in

L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\im

\displaystyle(\langle\OpW(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(-i\mu_{2}))g,g\rangle)+C_{2}\mu_{2}\lVert\OpW(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(-i\mu_{2}))g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\langle\OpW(\im(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(-i\mu_{2}))+C_{2}\mu_{2}(\overline{z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(-i\mu_{2})})(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(-i\mu_{2})))g,g\rangle

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle-c\mu_{2}\hbar^{\delta}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{2}+\im(z))\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}-\tilde{c}(\hbar^{\delta}+\mu_{2}\hbar^{\delta})\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}\geq\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{6}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2},

for
\hbar
sufficiently small. Moreover, by a Hölder inequality we have

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{6}

\displaystyle\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\left|\langle\OpW(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(-i\mu_{2}))g,g\rangle\right|+C_{2}\mu_{2}\lVert\OpW(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(-i\mu_{2}))g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{12}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}+(\frac{6}{2c_{0}\mu_{2}}+C_{2}\mu_{2})\lVert\OpW(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(-i\mu_{2}))g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{2}.

This shows that there exists a constant
C
such

\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{C}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\lVert\OpW(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(-i\mu_{2}))g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

for all
g
in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Since

\OpW(z-\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon,0}(-i\mu_{2}))
is the principal part
of
\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;-i\mu_{2})
and the rest comes with
an extra
\hbar
in front as we have assumed regularity
\tau\geq 1

the above estimate imply

\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{2C}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\lVert z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;-i\mu_{2})g\rVert_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

for
\hbar
sufficiently small. We can do the above argument again
for
\im(z)\geq 0
and obtain the same result. The estimate implies
that the set
\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,\im(z)>-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4}\}
is in
the regularity set of
\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;-i\mu_{2})
. Since

\{z\in\mathbb{C}\,|\,\im(z)>-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4}\}
is connected we have
that this is a subset of the resolvent set if just one point of the
set is in the resolvent set. For a
z
in
\mathbb{C}
with positive
imaginary part and

\left|z\right|\geq 2\lVert\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;-i\mu_{2})\rVert
we have
existence of
(z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;-i\mu_{2}))^{-1}
as a Neumann
series. Hence we can conclude that

(z-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar;-i\mu_{2}))^{-1}
extends to a
holomorphic function for
z
in
\mathbb{C}
such

\im(z)\geq-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4C_{1}}
. This implies

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}}\psi_{-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4C_{1}}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\bar{\partial}\eta(-i\mu_{2},z)\,L(dz)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{C}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}}\psi_{-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4C_{1}}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\eta(-i\mu_{2},z)\,L(dz)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\im(z)\geq 0}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\eta(-i\mu_{2},z)\,L(dz)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\im(z)<0}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}}\psi_{-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4C_{1}}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\eta(-i\mu_{2},z)\,L(dz),

where we have used that
\psi_{-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4C_{1}}}(z)=1
for all

z
in
\mathbb{C}
with
\im(z)\geq 0
. This equality gives us the following
rewriting of (
5.34
)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\pi}

\displaystyle\int_{\im(z)>\mu_{1}}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\eta(-i\mu_{2},z)\,L(dz)

(5.35)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\im(z)<0}\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}}\psi_{-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4C_{1}}})(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\eta(-i\mu_{2},z)\,L(dz)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{N_{0}}),

for any
N_{0}
in
\mathbb{N}
. We have

\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}}\psi_{-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4C_{1}}})(z)=\bar{\partial}(\tilde{f})(z)(\psi_{\mu_{1}}\psi_{-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4C_{1}}})(z)+\tilde{f}\psi_{\mu_{1}}(z)\bar{\partial}\psi_{-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4C_{1}}}(z),

for
\im(z)<0
, where we have used that
\psi_{\mu_{1}}(z)=1
for

\im(z)\leq 1
. The part of the integral on the right hand side of
(
5.35
) with the derivative on
\tilde{f}
will
be small due to the same argumentation as previously in the
proof. What remains is the part where the derivative is on

\psi_{-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4C_{1}}}
. For this part we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\pi}\Big|

\displaystyle\int_{\im(z)<0}\tilde{f}(z)\bar{\partial}\psi_{-\frac{c_{0}\mu_{2}}{4C_{1}}}(z)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi_{\xi}](s-z)\eta(-i\mu_{2},z)\,L(dz)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\hbar^{d}\left(\frac{M\hbar}{C_{1}\xi}\log(\frac{1}{\hbar})\right)^{2}}\int_{\begin{subarray}{c}-\eta<\re(z)<\eta\\
-\frac{M\hbar c_{0}}{2C_{1}^{2}\xi}\log(\frac{1}{\hbar})<\im(z)<-\frac{M\hbar c_{0}}{4C_{1}^{2}\xi}\log(\frac{1}{\hbar})\end{subarray}}\frac{\xi}{\hbar}e^{\frac{\xi\im(z)}{2\hbar}}\,L(dz)

\displaystyle=\frac{C}{\hbar^{d}\left(\frac{M\hbar}{C_{1}\xi}\log(\frac{1}{\hbar})\right)^{2})}e^{-\frac{c_{o}M}{2C_{1}^{2}}\log(\frac{1}{\hbar})}=\frac{\tilde{C}\xi^{2}}{\hbar^{d+2}M^{2}\log(\frac{1}{\hbar})^{2}}\hbar^{\frac{c_{o}}{2C_{1}^{2}}M}.

Hence by choosing
M
sufficiently large we can make the above
expression smaller than
\hbar^{N}
for any
N
in
\mathbb{N}
. This
concludes the proof.
∎

This Theorem actually imply a stronger version of it self, where
the assumption of boundedness is not needed.

Corollary 5.6
.

Let
A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
be a strongly

\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible operator of regularity
\tau\geq 1

which satisfies Assumption
4.1
and assume there
exists a
\delta
in
(0,1)
such that

\varepsilon\geq\hbar^{1-\delta}
. Suppose there exists a number

\eta>0
such
a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}([-2\eta,2\eta])
is compact
and a constant
c>0
such

\left|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)\right|\geq c\quad\text{for all }(x,p)\in a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}([-2\eta,2\eta]),

where
a_{\varepsilon,0}
is the principal symbol of

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. Let
f
be in
C_{0}^{\infty}((-\eta,\eta))
and

\theta
be in
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{p})
such that

\supp(\theta)\subset a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}((-\eta,\eta))
. There
exists a constant
T_{0}>0
such that if
\chi
is in

C_{0}^{\infty}((\frac{1}{2}\hbar^{1-\gamma},T_{0}))
for a
\gamma
in

(0,\delta]
, then for every
N
in
\mathbb{N}
, we have

\left|\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta)]\right|\leq C_{N}\hbar^{N},

uniformly for
s
in
(-\eta,\eta)
.

Proof.

The operator
A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
satisfies the assumptions of
Theorem
4.11
. This gives us the functional calculus for
the pseudo-differential operator for functions in the set

\mathcal{W}
which contains all functions from
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
. It
can be remarked that the function

f(t)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi](s-t)
is a
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
in

t
and both imaginary and real part is also in
C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{R})
just with real values. By
g(t)
we denote either the real or imaginary part of

f(t)\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi](s-t)
. Theorem
4.11

gives

g(A_{\varepsilon}(h))=\sum_{j\geq 0}\hbar^{j}\OpW(a_{\varepsilon,j}^{g}),

where

\displaystyle a_{\varepsilon,0}^{g}

\displaystyle=g(a_{\varepsilon,0})

(5.36)

\displaystyle a_{\varepsilon,j}^{g}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2j-1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}d_{\varepsilon,j,k}g^{(k)}(a_{\varepsilon,0})\quad\quad\text{for $j\geq 1$},

the symbols
d_{\varepsilon,j,k}
are the polynomials from
Lemma
4.4
. Now Let
\varphi
be in

C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{p})
such
\varphi(x,p)=1
on a
neighbourhood of

\supp(f(a_{\varepsilon,0})\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi](s-a_{\varepsilon,0}))
. Then if we
define the operator
\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
as the operator
with symbol

\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)=\sum_{j\geq 0}\hbar^{j}\varphi a_{\varepsilon,j}.

This operator satisfies the assumptions in
Theorem
5.4
. Hence we have

\left|\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\chi](s-\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta)]\right|\leq C_{N}\hbar^{N}

(5.37)

But by construction we have

\lVert g(A_{\varepsilon}(h))-g(\tilde{A}_{\varepsilon}(h))\rVert\leq C_{n}\hbar^{n},

for every
n
in
\mathbb{N}
, where we have used the form of the symbols
given in (
5.36
). Combining this with
(
5.37
) we achieve the desired estimate.
∎

6
Weyl law for rough pseudo-differential operators

In this section we will prove a Weyl law for rough pseudo-differential
operators and we will do it with the approach used in

[
20
]
. Hence we will first consider some asymptotic
expansions of certain integrals.

Theorem 6.1
.

Let
A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
be a
\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible
operator of regularity
\tau\geq 1
which satisfies
Assumption
4.1
and assume there exists a
\delta

in
(0,1)
such that
\varepsilon\geq\hbar^{1-\delta}
. Suppose
there exists
\eta>0
such that

a_{0,\varepsilon}^{-1}([-2\eta,2\eta])
is compact and every value in the interval
[-2\eta,2\eta]
is non critical for
a_{0,\varepsilon}
, where

a_{\varepsilon,0}
is the principal symbol of

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. Let
\chi
be in
C^{\infty}_{0}((-T_{0},T_{0}))

and
\chi=1
in a neighbourhood of
0
, where
T_{0}
is the number
from Corollary
5.6
. Then for every
f
in

C_{0}^{\infty}((-\eta,\eta))
we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar^{-1}}\chi(t)\,dt=(2\pi\hbar)^{1-d}\Big[\sum_{j=0}^{N_{0}}\hbar^{j}\xi_{j}(s)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{N})\Big].

where the error term is uniform with respect to

s\in(-\eta,\eta)
and the number
N_{0}
depends on the desired error. The functions
\xi_{j}(s)
are smooth functions in
s
and are given by

\xi_{0}(s)=f(s)\int_{\{a_{\varepsilon,0}=s\}}\frac{1}{\left|\nabla{a_{\varepsilon,0}}\right|}\,dS_{s},

\xi_{j}(s)=\sum_{k=1}^{2j-1}\frac{1}{k!}f(s)\partial^{k}_{s}\int_{\{a_{\varepsilon,0}=s\}}\frac{d_{\varepsilon,j,k}}{\left|\nabla{a_{\varepsilon,0}}\right|}\,dS_{s},

where the symbols
d_{\varepsilon,j,k}
are the polynomials from Lemma
4.4
. In particular we have

\displaystyle\xi_{1}(s)=-f(s)\partial_{s}\int_{\{a_{\varepsilon,0}=s\}}\frac{a_{\varepsilon,1}}{\left|\nabla{a_{\varepsilon,0}}\right|}\,dS_{s}.

Moreover, we have the a priori bounds

|\hbar^{j}\xi_{j}(s)|\leq\begin{cases}C&\text{if $j=0$}\\
C\hbar&\text{if $j=1$}\\
C\hbar^{1+\delta(j-2)}&\text{if $j=1$}.\end{cases}

(6.1)

Remark 6.2
.

Suppose we are in the setting of Theorem
6.1
. The statement of the theorem can be rephrased in terms of convolution of measures and a function. To see this let
f
be in
C_{0}^{\infty}((-\eta,\eta))
, for this function we can define the function

\displaystyle M_{f}^{0}(\omega;\hbar)

\displaystyle=\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\bm{1}_{(-\infty,\omega]}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]

\displaystyle=\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\bm{1}_{[0,\infty)}(\omega-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]=\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))(\omega-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))_{+}^{0}],

where
t_{+}=\max(0,t)
. We have that
M_{f}^{0}(\omega;\hbar)
is a monotonic increasing function hence it defines a measure in the natural way. If we consider the function

\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}(t)=\frac{1}{2\pi\hbar}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{it\hbar^{-1}s}\chi(s)\,ds,

then we have

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*dM_{f}^{0}(\cdot;\hbar)(s)=\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)\in\mathcal{P}}\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}(s-e_{j}(\hbar))f(e_{j}(\hbar))=\Tr[\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}(s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]

(6.2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\hbar}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar^{-1}}\chi(t)\,dt=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\Big[\sum_{j=0}^{N_{0}}\hbar^{j}\xi_{j}(s)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{N})\Big].

This formulation of the theorem will prove useful when we consider Riesz means.

The proof of the theorem is split in two parts. First is the existence
of the expansion proven by a stationary phase theorem. Next is the
form of the coefficients found by application of the functional
calculus developed earlier.

Proof.

In order to be in a situation, where we can apply the stationary
phase theorem we need to exchange the propagator with the
approximation of it constructed in
Section
5
. As the construction
required auxiliary localisation we need to introduce these. Let

\theta
be in
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{x}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{p})
such

\supp(\theta)\subset a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}((-\eta,\eta))
and

\theta(x,p)=1
for all
(x,p)
in

\supp(f(a_{\varepsilon,0}))
. Now by
Lemma
4.14
we have

\lVert(1-\OpW(\theta))f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}\rVert_{\Tr}\leq\lVert(1-\OpW(\theta))f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\rVert_{\Tr}\leq C_{N}\hbar^{N},

(6.3)

for every
N
in
\mathbb{N}
. Hence we have

|\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]-\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]|\leq C_{N}\hbar^{N},

for any
N
in
\mathbb{N}
. This implies the identity

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}\chi(t)\,dt

(6.4)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}\chi(t)\,dt+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{N}).

In order to use the results of
Section
5
we need also to localise in
time. To do this we let
\chi_{2}
be in
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such

\chi_{2}(t)=1
for
t
in

[-\frac{1}{2}\hbar^{1-\frac{\delta}{2}},\frac{1}{2}\hbar^{1-\frac{\delta}{2}}]
and

\supp(\chi_{2})\subset[-\hbar^{1-\frac{\delta}{2}},\hbar^{1-\frac{\delta}{2}}]
. With this
function we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}\chi(t)\,dt

(6.5)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}\chi_{2}(t)\chi(t)\,dt

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}(1-\chi_{2}(t))\chi(t)\,dt.

We will use the notation
\tilde{\chi}(t)=(1-\chi_{2}(-t))\chi(-t)
in
the following. We start by considering the second term. Here we introduce an extra localisation analogous to how we introduced the first. Using the
estimate in (
6.3
) and cyclicity of the trace
again we have

\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]=\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}\OpW(\theta)]+C_{N}\hbar^{N}.

Now by Corollary
5.6
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}\OpW(\theta)]e^{-its\hbar}\tilde{\chi}(-t)\,dt

(6.6)

\displaystyle=2\pi\hbar\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\mathcal{F}_{\hbar}^{-1}[\tilde{\chi}](s-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\OpW(\theta)]\leq\tilde{C}_{N}\hbar^{N},

uniformly in
s
in
[-\eta,\eta]
and any
N
in
\mathbb{N}
. What remains
in (
6.5
) is the first term. For this term we change the quantisation of the
localisation. By Corollary
3.20
we
obtain for any
N
in
\mathbb{N}

\OpW(\theta)=\Opdef_{\hbar,0}(\theta_{0}^{N})+\hbar^{N+1}R_{N}(\hbar),

where
R_{N}
is a bounded operator uniformly in
\hbar
since

\theta
is a non-rough symbol. Moreover we have

\theta_{0}^{N}(x,p)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}\frac{\hbar^{j}}{j!}\big(-\frac{1}{2}\big)^{j}(\nabla_{x}D_{p})^{j}\theta(x,p).

If we choose
N
sufficiently large (greater than or equal to
2
)
we can exchange
\OpW(\theta)
by
\Opdef_{\hbar,0}(\theta_{0}^{N})
plus a
negligible error. We will in the following omit the
N
on

\theta_{0}^{N}
. For the first term on the right hand side in
(
6.5
) we have

\left|t\right|\leq\hbar^{1-\frac{\delta}{2}}
. Now by
Theorem
5.1
there exists

U_{N}(t,\varepsilon,\hbar)
with integral kernel

\displaystyle K_{U_{N}}(x,y,t,\varepsilon,\hbar)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\,dp,

such that

\lVert\hbar\partial_{t}U_{N}(t,\varepsilon,\hbar)-iU_{N}(t,\varepsilon,\hbar)A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leq C\hbar^{N_{0}},

(6.7)

for
\left|t\right|\leq\hbar^{1-\frac{\delta}{2}}
and

U_{N}(0,\varepsilon,\hbar)=\Opdef_{\hbar,0}(\theta_{0})
. We emphasise that
the number
N
in the operator
U_{N}
is dependent on the error

N_{0}
. We observe that

\displaystyle|\Tr[\Opdef_{\hbar,0}(\theta_{0})e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]-\Tr[U_{N}(t,\varepsilon,\hbar)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]|

\displaystyle=|\Tr[\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{s}(U_{N}(t-s,\varepsilon,\hbar)e^{is\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\,ds]|

\displaystyle=|\Tr[\int_{0}^{t}(-(\partial_{t}U_{N})(t-s,\varepsilon,\hbar)+i\hbar^{-1}U_{N}(t-s,\varepsilon,\hbar)A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{is\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\,ds]|

\displaystyle\leq\hbar^{-1}\int_{0}^{t}\lVert\hbar\partial_{s}U_{N}(s,\varepsilon,\hbar)-iU_{N}(s,\varepsilon,\hbar)A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\lVert e^{i(t-s)\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\rVert_{\Tr}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq C_{N}\hbar^{N_{0}-d},

where we have used (
6.7
). By combining this
with (
6.5
) and (
6.6
)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}\chi_{2}(t)\chi(t)\,dt

(6.8)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[U_{N}(t,\varepsilon,\hbar)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]e^{-its\hbar}\chi_{2}(t)\chi(t)\,dt+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{N}).

Before we proceed we will change the quantisation of

f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))
. From Theorem
4.11
we have

f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))=\sum_{j\geq 0}\hbar^{j}\OpW(a_{\varepsilon,j}^{f}),

where

a_{\varepsilon,j}^{f}=\sum_{k=1}^{2j-1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}d_{\varepsilon,j,k}f^{(k)}(a_{\varepsilon,0}),

(6.9)

the symbols
d_{\varepsilon,j,k}
are the polynomials from
Lemma
4.4
. We choose a sufficiently
large
N
and consider the first
N
terms of the operator

f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))
. For each of these terms we can use
Corollary
3.20
and this yields

\OpW(a_{\varepsilon,j}^{f})=\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(a_{\varepsilon,j}^{f,M})+\hbar^{M+1}R_{M},

where
\hbar^{M+1}R_{M}
is a bounded by
C_{M}\hbar^{N}
in operator
norm. The symbol
a_{\varepsilon,j}^{f,M}
is given by

a_{\varepsilon,j}^{f,M}=\sum_{j=0}^{M}\frac{\hbar^{j}}{j!}\big(\frac{1}{2}\big)^{j}(\nabla_{x}D_{p})^{j}a_{\varepsilon,j}^{f}.

By chossing
N
sufficiently large we can exchange

f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))
by

\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{f,M})\coloneqq\sum_{j=0}^{N}\hbar^{j}\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(a_{\varepsilon,j}^{f,M}),

plus a negligible error as
U_{N}(t,\varepsilon,\hbar)
is trace
class. We will omit the
M
when writing

\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{f,M}
. Hence we have the equality

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}\chi_{2}(t)\chi(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[U_{N}(t,\varepsilon,\hbar)\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{f,M})]e^{-its\hbar}\chi_{2}(t)\chi(t)\,dt+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{N}).

As we have the non-critical assumption
Lemma
5.3
gives us that the trace in the above expression is
negligible for
\frac{1}{2}\hbar^{1-\frac{\delta}{2}}\leq|t|\leq T_{0}
.
Hence we can omit the
\chi_{2}(t)
in the expression and then
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[\OpW(\theta)f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}\chi_{2}(t)\chi(t)\,dt

(6.10)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[U_{N}(t,\varepsilon,\hbar)\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{f,M})]e^{-its\hbar}\chi(t)\,dt+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{N}).

The two operators
U_{N}(t,\varepsilon,\hbar)
and

\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{f,M})
are both given by kernels
and the composition of the operators has the kernel

\displaystyle K_{U_{N}(t,\varepsilon,\hbar)\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{f,M})}(x,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{i\hbar^{-1}\langle x-y,p\rangle}e^{it\hbar^{-1}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)}\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{f,M}(y,p)\,dp.

We can now calculate the trace and we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[U_{N}(t,\varepsilon,\hbar)\Opdef_{\hbar,1}(\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{f,M})]e^{-its\hbar}\chi(t)\,dt

(6.11)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}}\chi(t)e^{it\hbar^{-1}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)-s)}u(x,p,t,\hbar,\varepsilon)\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{f,M}(x,p)\,dxdpdt,

where

u(x,p,t,\hbar,\varepsilon)=\sum_{j=0}^{N}(it\hbar^{-1})^{j}u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon).

In order to evaluate the integral we will a stationary phase argument. We will use the theorem in
t
and one of the
p

coordinates after using a partition of unity according to

p
. By assumption we have that
\left|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon},0\right|>c
on
the support of
\theta
. Hence we can make a partition
\Omega_{j}

such that
\partial_{p_{j}}a_{\varepsilon}\neq 0
on
\Omega_{j}
and
with loss of generality we can assume that
\Omega_{j}
is
connected. To this partition we choose a partition of the unit
supported on each of the sets
\Omega_{j}
. When we have localised to
each of these sets the calculation will be identical with some
indicies changed. Hence we assume that

\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon}\neq 0
on the entire support of the
integrant. We will now make a change of variables in the integral in
the following way:

F:(x,p)\rightarrow(X,P)=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d},a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p),p_{2},\dots,p_{d}).

This transformation has the following jacobian matrix

DF=\begin{pmatrix}I_{d}&0_{d\times d}\\
\nabla_{x}a_{\varepsilon,0}^{t}&\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}^{t}\\
0_{d-1\times d+1}&I_{d-1}\end{pmatrix},

where
I_{d}
is the
d
-dimensional identity matrix,

\nabla_{x}a_{\varepsilon}^{t}
and
\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon}^{t}
are the
transposed of the respective gradients and the zeros are
corresponding matrices with only zeroes and the dimensions indicated
in the subscript. We note that

\det(DF)=\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0},

which is non zero by our assumptions. Hence the inverse map exists
and we will denote it by
F^{-1}
. For the inverse
we denote the part that gives
p
as a function of
(X,P)
by

F_{2}^{-1}
. By this change of variables we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}}\chi(t)e^{it\hbar^{-1}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)-s)}u(x,p,t,\hbar,\varepsilon)\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{f,M}(x,p)\,dxdpdt

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d+1}}\chi(t)e^{it\hbar^{-1}(P_{1}-s)}\frac{u(X,F_{2}^{-1}(X,P),t,\hbar,\varepsilon)\tilde{a}_{\varepsilon}^{f,M}(X,F_{2}^{-1}(X,P))}{\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(X,F_{2}^{-1}(X,P))}\,dXdPdt,

where we have omitted the prefactor
(2\pi\hbar)^{-d}
. If we preform the change of variables
\tilde{P}_{1}=P_{1}-s
we arrive at a situation we we
can apply quadratic stationary phase. Hence by stationary phase in the variables
\tilde{P}_{1}
and
t
,
(
6.4
), (
6.5
),
(
6.8
), (
6.10
) and
(
6.11
) we get

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}\chi(t)\,dt=(2\pi\hbar)^{1-d}\Big[\sum_{j=0}^{N_{0}}\hbar^{j}\xi_{j}(s)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{N})\Big],

(6.12)

uniformly for
s
in
(-\eta,\eta)
. The a priori bounds given in the Theorem also follows directly from this application of stationary phase combined with the estimates on
u_{j}(x,p,\hbar,\varepsilon)
from Theorem
5.1
. This ends the proof of the
existence of the expansion.

From the above expression we have that

\xi_{j}(s)
are smooth functions in
s
hence the above expression
defines a distribution on
C^{\infty}_{0}((-\eta,\eta))
. So in order to
find the expressions of the
\xi_{j}(s)
’s we consider the action of
the distribution. We let
\varphi
be in

C_{0}^{\infty}((-\eta,\eta))
and consider the expresion

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}\chi(t)\varphi(s)\,dtds.

(6.13)

Using that
f
is supported in the pure point spectrum of

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}\chi(t)\varphi(s)\,dtds

(6.14)

\displaystyle=\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{F}_{1}[\chi](\tfrac{s}{\hbar})\varphi(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)-s)\,ds],

where we have used Fubini’s theorem. That
f
is supported in the pure point spectrum follows from Theorem
4.12
. If we consider the integral in
the right hand side of (
6.14
) and let
\psi
be in

C_{0}^{\infty}((-2,2))
such that
\psi(t)=1
for
\left|t\right|\leq 1
we
have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{F}_{1}[\chi](\tfrac{s}{\hbar})\varphi(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)-s)\,ds={}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-its\hbar^{-1}}\chi(t)\psi(s)\varphi(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)-s)\,dsdt

(6.15)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-its\hbar^{-1}}\chi(t)(1-\psi(s))\varphi(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)-s)\,dsdt.

From the identity

\Big(\frac{i\hbar}{s}\Big)^{n}\partial_{t}^{n}e^{-its\hbar^{-1}}=e^{-its\hbar^{-1}},

integration by parts, the spectral theorem and that the function

(1-\psi(s))
is support on
\left|s\right|\geq 1
, we have that

\Big\lVert\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-its\hbar^{-1}}\chi(t)(1-\psi(s))\varphi(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)-s)\,dsdt\Big\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}=C_{N}\hbar^{N},

(6.16)

for any
N
in
\mathbb{N}
. Now for the first integral in the
right hand side of (
6.15
) we have by
Proposition
2.1
(Quadratic stationary phase)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle e^{-its\hbar^{-1}}\chi(t)\psi(s)\varphi(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)-s)\,dsdt

(6.17)

\displaystyle=2\pi\hbar\sum_{j=0}^{N}\hbar^{j}\frac{(i)^{j}}{j!}\chi^{(j)}(0)\varphi^{(j)}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))+\hbar^{N+1}R_{N+1}(\hbar),

where we have used that
\psi(0)=1
and
\psi^{(j)}(0)=0
for all

j\in\mathbb{N}
. Moreover, we have from
Theorem
2.1
the estimate

\left|R_{N+1}(\hbar)\right|\leq c\sum_{l+k=2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\chi^{(N+1+l)}(t)\partial_{s}^{N+1+k}\psi(s)\varphi(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)-s)|\,dtds.

As the integrants are supported on a compact set the integral will
be convergent and since
\varphi
is
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
we have by the
spectral theorem

\lVert R_{N+1}(\hbar)\rVert_{\mathcal{L}(L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))}\leq C.

(6.18)

If we now use that
\chi
is
1
in a neighbourhood of
0
and
combine (
6.14
)–(
6.18
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-its\hbar}\chi(t)\varphi(s)\,dtds

(6.19)

\displaystyle=2\pi\hbar\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\varphi(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]+C_{N}\hbar^{N}.

Since both
f
and
\varphi
are
C_{0}^{\infty}((-\eta,\eta))

functions we have by Theorem
4.13
the identity

\Tr[f(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\varphi(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\sum_{j=0}^{N}\hbar^{j}T_{j}(f\varphi,A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{N+1-d}).

(6.20)

From
Theorem
4.13
we have the exact form of the
terms
T_{j}(f\varphi,A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))
, which is given
by

T_{j}(f\varphi,A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))=\begin{cases}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(f\varphi)(a_{\varepsilon,0})\,dxdp&j=0\\
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{\varepsilon,1}(f\varphi)^{(1)}(a_{\varepsilon,0})\,dxdp&j=1\\
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\sum_{k=1}^{2j-1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}d_{\varepsilon,j,k}(f\varphi)^{(k)}(a_{\varepsilon,0})\,dxdp&j\geq 2,\end{cases}

where the symbols
d_{\varepsilon,j,k}
are the polynomials from Lemma
4.4
. If we combine (
6.12
), (
6.19
) and
(
6.20
) we get

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\xi_{j}(s)\varphi(s)\,ds=T_{j}(f\varphi,A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)).

If we consider
T_{0}(f\varphi,A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))
we
have

\displaystyle T_{0}(f\varphi,A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(f\varphi)(a_{\varepsilon,0})\,dxdp

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(\omega)\varphi(\omega)\int_{\{a_{\varepsilon,0}=\omega\}}\frac{1}{\left|\nabla{a_{\varepsilon,0}}\right|}\,dS_{\omega}d\omega,

where
S_{\omega}
is the euclidian surface measure on the surface in

\mathbb{R}_{x}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{p}^{d}
given by the equation

a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)=\omega
. If we now consider

T_{j}(f\check{\varphi},A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))
we have

\displaystyle T_{j}(f\varphi,A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\sum_{k=1}^{2j-1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}d_{\varepsilon,j,k}(f\varphi)^{(k)}(a_{\varepsilon,0})\,dxdp

(6.21)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2j-1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(f\varphi)^{(k)}(\omega)\int_{\{a_{\varepsilon,0}=\omega\}}\frac{d_{\varepsilon,j,k}}{\left|\nabla{a_{\varepsilon,0}}\right|}\,dS_{\omega}d\omega

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2j-1}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(f\varphi)(\omega)\partial^{k}_{\omega}\int_{\{a_{\varepsilon,0}=\omega\}}\frac{d_{\varepsilon,j,k}}{\left|\nabla{a_{\varepsilon,0}}\right|}\,dS_{\omega}d\omega,

where we in the last equality have made integration by parts. These equalities implies the stated form of the functions
\xi_{j}
.
∎

We will now fixing some notation which will be useful for the rest of
this section. We let
\chi
be a function in
C_{0}^{\infty}((-T_{0},T_{0}))
,
where the
T_{0}
is a sufficiently small number. The number will be the
number
T_{0}
from Corollary
5.6
. We suppose

\chi
is even and
\chi(t)=1
for
|t|\leq\frac{T_{0}}{2}
. We then set

\hat{\chi}_{1}(s)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(t)e^{-its}\,dt.

We assume
\hat{\chi}_{1}\geq 0
and that there is a
c>0
such that

\hat{\chi}_{1}(t)\geq c
in a small interval around
0
. These
assumptions can be guaranteed by replacing
\chi
by
\chi*\chi
. With
this we set

\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}(s)=\frac{1}{\hbar}\hat{\chi}_{1}(\tfrac{s}{\hbar})=\frac{1}{2\pi\hbar}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\chi(t)e^{its\hbar^{-1}}\,dt.

Lemma 6.3
.

Assume we are in the setting of Theorem
6.1
and let
g
be in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
with
\supp(g)\subset(-\eta,\eta)
. Then for any
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(s)\xi_{j}(s)\,ds\Big|\leq C\varepsilon^{(\tau-j)_{-}}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}.

Proof.

From Theorem
6.1
we have that
\xi_{j}(s)
is bounded for all
j
. Hence by standard approximations it is sufficient to prove the statement for
g\in C_{0}^{\infty}((-\eta,\eta))
which we assume from here on.

That
\xi_{j}(s)
is bounded for all
j
immediately give us the estimate for
j=0
. So we may assume
j\geq 1
. By definition of
\xi_{j}(s)
we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(s)\xi_{j}(s)\,ds

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2j-1}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(s)f(s)\partial^{k}_{s}\int_{\{a_{\varepsilon,0}=s\}}\frac{d_{\varepsilon,j,k}}{\left|\nabla{a_{\varepsilon,0}}\right|}\,dS_{s}ds

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2j-1}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{k!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(gf)^{(k)}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))d_{\varepsilon,j,k}(x,p)\,dxdp

On the support of
fg(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))
we have that
|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)|>c
. Hence we can make a partition
\{\Omega_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}
such that
\partial_{p_{j}}a_{\varepsilon}\neq 0
on
\Omega_{j}
and with loss of generality we can assume that
\Omega_{j}
is connected. As in the proof of Theorem
6.1
we now choose a partition of unity supported on each
\Omega_{j}
and split the integral accordingly. Hence without loss of generality we can assume
\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon}\neq 0
on the whole support. With the same notation as in the proof of Theorem
6.1
we preform the change of variables

F:(x,p)\rightarrow(X,P)=(x_{1},\dots,x_{d},a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p),p_{2},\dots,p_{d}).

This gives us that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(s)\xi_{j}(s)\,ds

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{2j-1}\frac{1}{k!}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}g(P_{1})f(P_{1})\partial_{P_{1}}^{k}\frac{d_{\varepsilon,j,k}(X,F_{2}^{-1}(X,P))}{\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}(X,F_{2}^{-1}(X,P))}\,dXdP,

where we after the change of variables have preformed integration by parts. We have from the definition of the polynomials
d_{\varepsilon,j,k}
that they are of regularity
\tau-j
and since we are integrating over a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
we obtain the estimate

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}}g(s)\xi_{j}(s)\,ds\Big|\leq C\varepsilon^{(\tau-j)_{-}}\lVert g\rVert_{L^{1}(\mathbb{R})}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

6.1
Weyl law for rough pseudo-differential operators

Before we state and prove the Weyl law for rough pseudo-differential operators we recall a Tauberian theorem from

[
20
, Theorem V–13]
.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}
and
\sigma_{\hbar}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a family
of increasing functions, where
\hbar
is in
(0,1]
. Suppose that

(i)

\sigma_{\hbar}(\tau)=0
for every
\tau\leq\tau_{1}
.

(ii)

\sigma_{\hbar}(\tau)
is constant for
\tau\geq\tau_{2}
.

(iii)

\sigma_{\hbar}(\tau)=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{-n})
as

\hbar\rightarrow 0
,
n\geq 1
and uniformly with respect to
\tau

in
\mathbb{R}
.

(iv)

\partial_{\tau}\sigma_{\hbar}*\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}(\tau)=\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{-n})
as
\hbar\rightarrow 0
, with the same
n

as above and uniformly with respect to
\tau
in
\mathbb{R}
.

where
\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}
is defined as above. Then we have

\sigma_{\hbar}(\tau)=\sigma_{\hbar}*\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}(\tau)+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-d}),

as
\hbar\rightarrow 0
and uniformly with respect to
\tau
in
\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem 6.5
.

Let
A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
be a strongly

\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible operator of regularity
\tau\geq 1

which satisfies Assumption
4.1
and assume there
exists a
\delta
in
(0,1)
such that

\varepsilon\geq\hbar^{1-\delta}
. Suppose there exists a
\eta>0

such
a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}((-\infty,\eta])
is compact, where

a_{\varepsilon,0}
is the principal symbol of

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. Moreover we suppose

|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)|\geq c\quad\text{for all }(x,p)\in a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}(\{0\}).

(6.22)

Then we have

|\Tr[\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))\,dxdp|\leq C\hbar^{1-d},

for all sufficiently small
\hbar
.

Proof.

By the assumption in (
6.22
) there exists a

\nu>0
such

|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)|\geq\frac{c}{2}\quad\text{for all }(x,p)\in a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}([-2\nu,2\nu]).

More over by Theorem
4.2
we have that the
spectrum of
A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
is bounded from below uniformly
in
\hbar
and let
E
denote a number with distance
1
to the
bottom of the spectrums. We now take two functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}

in
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such

f_{1}(t)+f_{2}(t)=1,

for every
t
in
[E,0]
,

\supp(f_{2})\subset[-\frac{\nu}{4},\frac{\nu}{4}]
,
f_{2}(t)=1
for

t
in
[-\frac{\nu}{8},\frac{\nu}{8}]
and
f_{2}(t)=f_{2}(-t)
for all

t
. With these functions we have

\Tr[\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]=\Tr[f_{1}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]+\Tr[f_{2}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))].

(6.23)

For the first term on the right hand in the above equality we have
by Theorem
4.13
that

\Tr[f_{1}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f_{1}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))\,dxdp+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-d}).

(6.24)

In order to calculate the second term on the right hand side in
(
6.23
) we will study the function

\omega\rightarrow M(\omega;\hbar)=\Tr[f_{2}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\bm{1}_{(-\infty,\omega]}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))].

(6.25)

We have that
M(\omega;\hbar)
satisfies the three first conditions
in Theorem
6.4
. We will in what follows use the notation

\mathcal{P}=\supp(f_{2})\cap\spec(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)),

where
\spec(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))
is the spectrum of the operator

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. The function
M
can be written in the
following form

M(\omega;\hbar)=\sum_{e_{j}\in\mathcal{P}}f_{2}(e_{j})\bm{1}_{[e_{j},\infty)}(\omega),

since
f_{2}
is supported in the pure point spectrum of

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. This follows from Theorem
4.12
. Let
\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}
be defined as
above. Then we will consider the convolution

(M(\cdot;\hbar)*\hat{\chi}_{\hbar})(\omega)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}M(s;\hbar)\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}(\omega-s)\,ds=\sum_{e_{j}\in\mathcal{P}}f_{2}(e_{j})\int_{e_{j}}^{\infty}\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}(\omega-s)\,ds.

If we take a derivative with respect to
\omega
we get

\displaystyle\partial_{\omega}(M(\cdot;\hbar)*\hat{\chi}_{\hbar})(\omega)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{j}\in\mathcal{P}}f_{2}(e_{j})\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}(\omega-e_{j})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi\hbar}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Tr[f_{2}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))e^{it\hbar^{-1}A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)}]e^{-it\omega\hbar^{-1}}\chi(t)\,dt,

by the definition of
\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}
. We get now by
Theorem
6.1
the identity

\partial_{\omega}(M(\cdot;\hbar)*\hat{\chi}_{\hbar})(\omega)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}f_{2}(\omega)\int_{\{a_{\varepsilon,0}=\omega\}}\frac{1}{\left|\nabla a_{\varepsilon,0}\right|}\,dS_{\omega}+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-d}),

(6.26)

where the size of the error follows from the a priori estimates given in the Theorem. This verifies the fourth condition in Theorem
6.4
for

M(\cdot;\hbar)
, hence the Theorem gives the identity

\displaystyle\Tr[f_{2}

\displaystyle(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]

(6.27)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}f_{2}(\omega)\int_{\{a_{\varepsilon,0}=\omega\}}\frac{1}{\left|\nabla a_{\varepsilon,0}\right|}\,dS_{\omega}d\omega+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-d})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}f_{2}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))\,dxdp+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-d}).

By combining (
6.23
), (
6.24
) and
(
6.27
) we get

\Tr[\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]=\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))\,dxdp+\mathcal{O}(\hbar^{1-d}).

This is the desired estimate and this ends the proof.
∎

6.2
Riesz means for rough pseudo-differential operators

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
be a strongly

\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible operator of regularity
\tau\geq 2

which satisfies Assumption
4.1
and assume there
exists a
\delta
in
(0,1)
such that

\varepsilon\geq\hbar^{1-\delta}
. Suppose there exists a
\eta>0

such
a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}((-\infty,\eta])
is compact, where

a_{\varepsilon,0}
is the principal symbol of

A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. Moreover we suppose

|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)|\geq c\quad\text{for all }(x,p)\in a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}(\{0\}).

(6.28)

Then for
1\geq\gamma>0
we have

\big|\Tr[(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))_{-}^{\gamma}]-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}[\Psi_{0}(\gamma,A_{\varepsilon})+\hbar\Psi_{1}(\gamma,A_{\varepsilon})]\big|\leq C\hbar^{1+\gamma-d},

for all sufficiently small
\hbar
. The numbers
\Psi_{j}(\gamma,A_{\varepsilon})
are given by

\displaystyle\Psi_{0}(\gamma,A_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))_{-}^{\gamma}\,dxdp

(6.29)

\displaystyle\Psi_{1}(\gamma,A_{\varepsilon})

\displaystyle=\gamma\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{\varepsilon,1}(x,p)(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))_{-}^{\gamma-1}\,dxdp.

Remark 6.7
.

The proof of this theorem is valid for any
\gamma>0
. For the case where
\gamma>1
the expansion will have additional terms. These additional terms can also be found and calculated explicitly. But note that to ensure the error is as stated here one will need to impose the restriction
\gamma\leq\tau-1
on
\gamma
and the regularity
\tau
in genereal.

If we had assumed
\tau=1
the error would, in terms of the semiclassical parameter, have been
