\max(\hbar^{1+\delta-d},\hbar^{1+\gamma-d})
. Under the assumption that
\delta\geq\gamma
we would get the desired error of
\hbar^{1+\gamma-d}
. This choice would be a possibility, however if
\tau=1
and we have such a choice of
\delta
will only be able to obtain the desired estimate, when comparing the phase space integrals in the proof of Theorem
1.5
, in the case
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{3}
. In this case we would need to assume
\mu\geq\frac{2\gamma}{1-\gamma}
. For
\gamma>\frac{1}{3}
we will not be able to obtain the desired error when comparing the phase space integrals in the proof of Theorem
1.5
due to the fraction
\frac{2\gamma}{1-\gamma}
being greater than one.

Proof.

By the assumption in (
6.28
) there exists a

\nu>0
such

|\nabla_{p}a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)|\geq\frac{c}{2}\quad\text{for all }(x,p)\in a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}([-2\nu,2\nu]).

By Theorem
4.2
we have that the
spectrum of
A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
is bounded from below uniformly
in
\hbar
. We will in the following let
E
denote a number with distance
1
to the
bottom of the spectrum. We now take two functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}

in
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
such

f_{1}(t)+f_{2}(t)=1,

for every
t
in
[E,0]
,

\supp(f_{2})\subset[-\frac{\nu}{4},\frac{\nu}{4}]
,
f_{2}(t)=1
for

t
in
[-\frac{\nu}{8},\frac{\nu}{8}]
and
f_{2}(t)=f_{2}(-t)
for all

t
. We can now write

\Tr[(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))_{-}^{\gamma}]=\Tr[f_{1}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))_{-}^{\gamma}]+\Tr[f_{2}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))_{-}^{\gamma}]

(6.30)

The first term in (
6.30
) is now the trace of a smooth compactly supported function of our operator. Hence we can calculate the asymptotic using Theorem
4.13
. Before applying this theorem we will study the second term in (
6.30
). Before we proceed we note that

(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))_{-}^{\gamma}=(0-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))_{+}^{\gamma}

This form will be slightly more convient to work with and we will introduce the notation
\varphi^{\gamma}(t)=(t)_{+}^{\gamma}
.
As we will use a smoothing procedure we will consider the expression

\displaystyle M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega;\hbar)

\displaystyle=\Tr[f_{2}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))\varphi^{\gamma}(\omega-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))]

(6.31)

\displaystyle=\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)\leq\omega}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))\varphi^{\gamma}(\omega-e_{j}(\hbar))=(\varphi^{\gamma}*dM_{f_{2}}^{0}(\cdot;\hbar))(\omega),

where
dM_{f_{2}}^{0}
1
1

1

The measure is just a sum of delta measures in the eigenvalues.
is the measure induced by the function

M_{f_{2}}^{0}(\omega;\hbar)=\Tr[f_{2}(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))(\omega-A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))_{+}^{0}],

as in Remark
6.2
. Let
\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}(t)
be as above and consider the convolution
\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega)=[\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*\varphi^{\gamma}*dM_{f_{2}}^{0}(\cdot;\hbar)](\omega)=[\varphi^{\gamma}*\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*dM_{f_{2}}^{0}(\cdot;\hbar)](\omega).

From Remark
6.2
we have an asymptotic expansion of
\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*dM_{f_{2}}^{0}(\cdot;\hbar)(\omega)
. In order to see that we can use this expansion let
g
be in
C_{0}^{\infty}([-\nu,\nu])
such that
g(t)=1
for all
t
in
[-\frac{\nu}{2},\frac{\nu}{2}]
. Then we have

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle[\varphi^{\gamma}*\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*dM_{f_{2}}^{0}(\cdot;\hbar)](\omega)

(6.32)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\gamma}(\omega-t)g(t)[\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*dM_{f_{2}}^{0}(\cdot;\hbar)](t)\,dt+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\gamma}(\omega-t)(1-g(t))[\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*dM_{f_{2}}^{0}(\cdot;\hbar)](t)\,dt

For the second term we have

(1-g(t))[\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*dM_{f_{2}}^{0}(\cdot;\hbar)](t)=\frac{1-g(t)}{2\pi\hbar}\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))\hat{\chi}\left(\tfrac{e_{j}(\hbar)-t}{\hbar}\right),

Since
\hat{\chi}
is Schwarz class and
\left|t-e_{j}(\hbar)\right|\geq\frac{\nu}{4}
on the support of
(1-g(t))
by the support properties of
f_{2}
we have that

\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\gamma}(\omega-t)(1-g(t))[\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*dM_{f_{2}}^{0}(\cdot;\hbar)](t)\,dt\right|\leq C_{N}\hbar^{N}

(6.33)

for all
N
in
\mathbb{N}
. We can use the expansion from Remark
6.2
on the first term in (
6.32
) due to the support properties of
g
and the estimate obtained in (
6.33
) for the second. This yields the estimate

\displaystyle\Big|[\varphi^{\gamma}*\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*dM_{f_{2}}^{0}(\cdot;\hbar)](\omega)-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\sum_{j=0}^{1}\hbar^{j}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\varphi^{\gamma}(\omega-t)g(t)\xi_{j}(t)\,dt\Big|\leq C\hbar^{2-d},

(6.34)

where we have also used Lemma
6.3
and the assumption that
\tau\geq 2
to obtain the estimate. Combining (
6.30
), (
6.31
) and (
6.34
) we obtain that

\displaystyle\big|\Tr[(A_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))_{-}^{\gamma}]-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}

\displaystyle[\Psi_{0}(\gamma,A_{\varepsilon})+\hbar\Psi_{1}(\gamma,A_{\varepsilon})]\big|

\displaystyle\leq|M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega;\hbar)-[\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\cdot;\hbar)](\omega)|+C\hbar^{2-d}.

What remains is an Tauberian argument to prove that

|M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega;\hbar)-[\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\cdot;\hbar)](\omega)|\leq C\hbar^{1+\gamma-d}

uniform in
\omega
. We have by definition of
\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}
that

M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega;\hbar)-[\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\cdot;\hbar)](\omega)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega;\hbar)-M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega-t\hbar;\hbar))\hat{\chi}(t)\,dt,

where we have used that

\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{\chi}(t)\,dt=1.

In order to proceed we will need the Fourier transform of
M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega;\hbar)
. This function exists as a limit of a complex Fourier transform. We have that

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\cdot;\hbar)](t-i\kappa)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i(t-i\kappa)s}\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))\varphi^{\gamma}(s-e_{j}(\hbar))\,ds

(6.35)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-i(t-i\kappa)s}\varphi^{\gamma}(s)\,ds\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}e^{-i(t-i\kappa)e_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{(-it-\kappa)s}s^{\gamma}\,ds\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}e^{-i(t-i\kappa)e_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))

\displaystyle=\frac{\Gamma(\gamma+1)}{(t-i\kappa)^{\gamma+1}}i^{\gamma+1}\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}e^{-i(t-i\kappa)e_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar)),

Where
\Gamma
is the gamma-function. By taking
\kappa
to zero we get that

\mathcal{F}[M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\cdot;\hbar)](t)=\frac{\Gamma(\gamma+1)}{(t+i0)^{\gamma+1}}e^{\frac{i\pi}{2}(\gamma+1)}\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}e^{-ite_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))

(6.36)

With this we have

M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega;\hbar)-M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega-t\hbar;\hbar)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{is\omega}\mathcal{F}[M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\cdot;\hbar)](s)(1-e^{-it\hbar s})\,ds

Combining these things we have

\displaystyle M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega;\hbar)-[\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\cdot;\hbar)](\omega)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega;\hbar)-M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega-t\hbar;\hbar))\hat{\chi}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{is\omega}\mathcal{F}[M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\cdot;\hbar)](s)(1-e^{-it\hbar s})\hat{\chi}(t)\,dsdt

By reintroducing
\kappa
and then take
\kappa
to zero we get by Fubini and the form of the Fourier transform that

\displaystyle M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\omega;\hbar)-[\hat{\chi}_{\hbar}*M_{f_{2}}^{\gamma}(\cdot;\hbar)](\omega)

\displaystyle=\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{is\omega}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma+1)}{(s-i\kappa)^{\gamma+1}}{i^{\gamma+1}}\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}e^{-i(s-i\kappa)e_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar)))(1-e^{-it\hbar s})\hat{\chi}(t)\,dsdt

\displaystyle=\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow 0}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{is\omega}\frac{\Gamma(\gamma+1)}{(s-i\kappa)^{\gamma+1}}{i^{\gamma+1}}\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}e^{-i(s-i\kappa)e_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))(1-\chi(-\hbar s))\,ds

\displaystyle=C(\gamma)\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))\hbar^{-1}\lim_{\kappa\rightarrow 0}e^{-i\kappa e_{j}(\hbar)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{is\hbar^{-1}(\omega-e_{j}(\hbar))}\frac{(1-\chi(s))}{(s\hbar^{-1}-i\kappa)^{\gamma+1}}\,ds

\displaystyle=C(\gamma)\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))\hbar^{\gamma}\int_{\mathbb{R}}e^{-is\hbar^{-1}(e_{j}(\hbar)-\omega)}\frac{(1-\chi(s))}{s^{\gamma+1}}\,ds

\displaystyle=C(\gamma)\hbar^{\gamma}\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))\hat{\psi}\left(\frac{e_{j}(\hbar)-\omega}{\hbar}\right),

where
\psi(s)=(1-\chi(s))s^{-\gamma-1}
which is in
L^{1}(\mathbb{R})
by the definition of
\chi
and that
\gamma>0
. The function
\hat{\psi}
will be continuous and smooth except at
0
, moreover it will decay at any polynomial order at infinity. Now we just need to estimate the sum such that we get one power of
\hbar
and loos
d
. Too do too note that

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{e_{j}(\hbar)}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))\hat{\psi}\left(\frac{e_{j}(\hbar)-\omega}{\hbar}\right)\Big|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\sum_{m\hbar<e_{j}(\hbar)-\omega\leq(m+1)\hbar}f_{2}(e_{j}(\hbar))\hat{\psi}\left(\frac{e_{j}(\hbar)-\omega}{\hbar}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}C\hbar^{1-d}(1+m^{2})\sup_{m<t\leq m+1}|\hat{\psi}(t)|\leq C\hbar^{1-d}

where we really need that
f_{2}
is supported in a non-critical interval. Hence we get a Tauberian error of the desired order.
∎

7
Proof of main theorems

Lemma 7.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}
be a sesquilinear form which satisfies Assumption
1.2
with the numbers
(\tau,\mu)
in
\mathbb{N}\times[0,1]
. Suppose
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
is the framing sesquilinear forms constructed in Proposition
2.5
. Then for all
\varepsilon
sufficiently small there exists two
\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible operators of regularity
\tau

A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
such that they satisfies Assumption
4.1
. In particular they will be lower semi-bounded, selfajoint for all
\hbar
sufficiently small and satisfies that

A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)[\varphi,\psi]=\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\varepsilon}^{\pm}[\varphi,\psi]\qquad\text{for all $\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\varepsilon}^{\pm})$ }.

(7.1)

Remark 7.2
.

Assume we are in the setting of Lemma
7.1
. Since we have that
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
both are
\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible of regularity
\tau
it follows from Theorem
3.18
that we can write the operators as a sum Weyl quantised symbols that is

A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)=\sum_{j=0}^{2m}\hbar^{j}\OpW(a^{\pm}_{\varepsilon,j}).

(7.2)

Written in this form the operator still satisfies Assumption
4.1
. In particular we have that

a^{\pm}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)=\sum_{|\alpha|,|\beta|\leq m}a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)p^{\alpha+\beta}\pm C_{1}\varepsilon^{k+\mu}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}p^{2\alpha}

(7.3)

and

a^{\pm}_{\varepsilon,1}(x,p)=i\sum_{|\alpha|,|\beta|\leq m}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\beta_{j}-\alpha_{j}}{2}\partial_{x_{j}}a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)p^{\alpha+\beta-\eta_{j}},

(7.4)

where
\eta_{j}
is the multi index with all zeroes except on the
j
’th coordinate where it is 1. Note that from the assumption
a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)=\overline{a_{\beta\alpha}^{\varepsilon}(x)}
it follows that
a^{\pm}_{\varepsilon,1}(x,p)
is real valued.

Proof.

Assume
\varphi
and
\psi
are Schwartz functions and recall that the sesquilinear forms
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\varepsilon}^{\pm}[\varphi,\psi]={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|,\left|\beta\right|\leq m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)(\hbar D_{x})^{\beta}\varphi(x)\overline{(\hbar D_{x})^{\alpha}\psi(x)}\,dx

(7.5)

\displaystyle\pm C_{1}\varepsilon^{k+\mu}\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\hbar D_{x})^{\alpha}\varphi(x)\overline{(\hbar D_{x})^{\alpha}\psi(x)}\,dx

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|,\left|\beta\right|\leq m}\sum_{\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha}(-i\hbar)^{|\alpha_{1}|}\binom{\alpha}{\alpha_{1}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}[\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}](x)(\hbar D_{x})^{\alpha+\beta-\alpha_{1}}\varphi(x)\overline{\psi(x)}\,dx

\displaystyle\pm C_{1}\varepsilon^{k+\mu}\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|\leq m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(\hbar D_{x})^{2\alpha}\varphi(x)\overline{\psi(x)}\,dx,

where
a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)
are smooth functions and in the identity we have made integration by parts. With this in mind we define the symbol
b_{\varepsilon}(x,p;\hbar)
to be

\displaystyle b_{\varepsilon}(x,p)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{m}(-i\hbar)^{j}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\leq|\alpha|\leq m\\
|\beta|\leq m\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha\\
|\alpha_{1}|=j\end{subarray}}\binom{\alpha}{\alpha_{1}}[\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}](x)p^{\alpha+\beta-\alpha_{1}}\pm C_{1}\varepsilon^{k+\mu}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}p^{2\alpha}

(7.6)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{m}\hbar^{j}b_{\varepsilon,j}(x,p).

Next we verify that there exists a
\zeta<0
such that
b_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)-\zeta
is a tempered weight and that
b_{\varepsilon,j}
is in
\Gamma^{b_{\varepsilon,0}-\zeta,\tau-j}_{0,\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}_{x}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{p}^{d})
for all
j
.

We have that the forms are elliptic for all
\varepsilon
sufficiently small and by assumption all coefficients are bounded below by a number
-\zeta_{0}
. This implies that

\displaystyle b_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\alpha|,|\beta|\leq m}a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)p^{\alpha+\beta}\pm C_{1}\varepsilon^{k+\mu}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}p^{2\alpha}

(7.7)

\displaystyle\geq C|p|^{2m}-\zeta_{0}\sum_{|\alpha|,|\beta|<m}|p^{\alpha+\beta}|-C_{1}\varepsilon^{k+\mu}\sum_{|\alpha|<m}|p^{2\alpha}|\geq-C

for some positive constant
C
and for all
(x,p)
. Here we have used that
a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)=\overline{a_{\beta\alpha}^{\varepsilon}(x)}
which implies that
b_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)
is real for all
(x,p)
. Hence we can find
\zeta<0
such that
b_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)-\zeta>0
for all
(x,p)
.

To see that this is a tempered weight we first recall how the new coefficients
a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)
was constructed. They are given by

a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a_{\alpha\beta}(x-\varepsilon y)\omega(y)\,dy,

where
\omega(y)
is a real Schwarz function integrating to 1. By assumption there exists a constant
c
such that
a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)+c>0
and we have

\displaystyle a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)+c=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(a_{\alpha\beta}(x-\varepsilon y)+c)\omega(y)\,dy\leq C_{1}(a_{\alpha\beta}(z)+c)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+|x-\varepsilon y-z|)^{M}\omega(y)\,dy

(7.8)

\displaystyle\leq C(a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(z)+a_{\alpha\beta}(z)-a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(z)+c)(1+|x-z|)^{M}\leq\tilde{C}(a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(z)+c)(1+|x-z|)^{M},

where we have used that
\varepsilon\leq 1
, some of the assumptions on
a_{\alpha\beta}(x)
and that

|a_{\alpha\beta}(z)-a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(z)|\leq c\varepsilon^{1+\mu},

(7.9)

by Proposition
2.4
. This gives us that the coefficients
a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)
have at most polynomial growth. Due to the polynomial structure of
b_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)
and the at most polynomial growth of
a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)
there exists constants
C
and
N
independent of
\varepsilon
in
(0,1]
such that for all
(x,p)
in
\mathbb{R}^{2d}

b_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)-\zeta\leq C(b_{\varepsilon,0}(y,q)-\zeta)\lambda(x-y,p-q)^{N}\qquad\forall(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}.

(7.10)

This shows that
b_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)
is a tempered weight. To see that
b_{\varepsilon,j}
is in
\Gamma^{b_{\varepsilon,0}-\zeta,\tau-j}_{0,\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}_{x}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{p}^{d})
for all
j
we first note that for
\eta
in
\mathbb{N}^{d}_{0}
with
|\eta|\leq\tau
we have

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{x}^{\eta}a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|

\displaystyle=|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{x}^{\eta}a_{\alpha\beta}(x-\varepsilon y)\omega(y)\,dy|\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\partial_{x}^{\eta}a_{\alpha\beta}(x-\varepsilon y)\omega(y)|\,dy

(7.11)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}c_{\eta}(a_{\alpha\beta}(x-\varepsilon y)+c)|\omega(y)|\,dy\leq C(a_{\alpha\beta}(x)+c)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}(1+\varepsilon|y|)^{M}|\omega(y)|\,dy

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}(a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)+c),

where we again have used (
7.9
). The calculation also shows that
C_{j}
is uniform for
\varepsilon
in
(0,1]
. For any
\eta

in
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
with
\left|\eta\right|\geq\tau
we have

\left|\partial_{x}^{\eta}a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)\right|\leq c\varepsilon^{\tau+\mu-\left|\eta\right|}\leq C_{\eta}\varepsilon^{\tau-\left|\eta\right|}(a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)+c),

(7.12)

by Proposition
2.4
with a constant which is uniform for
\varepsilon
in
(0,1]
. From combing (
7.11
) and (
7.12
) it follows that we for all
\eta
and
\delta
in
\mathbb{N}_{0}^{d}
can find constants
C_{\eta\delta}
such that

|\partial_{x}^{\eta}\partial_{p}^{\delta}b_{\varepsilon,j}(x,p)|\leq C_{\eta\delta}\varepsilon^{-(\tau-j-|\eta|)_{-}}(b_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)-\zeta)\qquad\forall(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}.

(7.13)

This estimate gives us that
b_{\varepsilon,j}
is in
\Gamma^{b_{\varepsilon,0}-\zeta,\tau-j}_{0,\varepsilon}(\mathbb{R}_{x}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{p}^{d})
for all
j
.

Define the operators
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
to be

A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)=\Opdef_{\hbar,0}(b_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))

(7.14)

From the above estimates we have that
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
is a
\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible of regularity
\tau
. That the regularity is
\tau
follows directly from Proposition
2.4
. Further more we already have that the operator
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
satisfies

(

⁢
i
i

)

and

(

⁢
i
i
i

)

from Assumption
4.1
. From (
7.5
) we have that
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
also satisfies

(
i
)

of Assumption
4.1
. Theorem
4.2
now gives us that
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
are lower semi-bounded and essentially self-adjointfor all
\hbar
sufficiently small. Denote also by
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
the self-adjointexstension. From (
7.5
) we also get that

A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)[\varphi,\psi]=\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\varepsilon}^{\pm}[\varphi,\psi]\qquad\text{for all $\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\varepsilon}^{\pm})$ }.

(7.15)

This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}
be a sesquilinear form which satisfies Assumption
1.2
with the numbers
(\tau,\mu)
in
\mathbb{N}\times[0,1]
. Then the form is symmetric, lower semi-bounded, densely defined and closed.

Proof.

Recall that the sesquilinear form
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}
is given by

\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}[\varphi,\psi]=\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|,\left|\beta\right|\leq m}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}a_{\alpha\beta}(x)(\hbar D_{x})^{\beta}\varphi(x)\overline{(\hbar D_{x})^{\alpha}\psi(x)}\,dx,\qquad\varphi,\psi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}).

(7.16)

We have by assumption that
a_{\alpha\beta}(x)=\overline{a_{\beta\alpha}(x)}
for all
\alpha
and
\beta
. This gives us that the form is symmetric. Moreover, we also have that the coefficients
a_{\alpha\beta}(x)
at most grow polynomially, hence it follows that the Schwartz functions are contained in the form domain. This gives that the form is densely defined.

From Proposition
2.5
we get the two forms
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
such that

\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\varepsilon}^{-}[\varphi,\varphi]\leq\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}[\varphi,\varphi]\leq\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\varepsilon}^{+}[\varphi,\varphi]

(7.17)

for all
\varphi
in
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{\hbar})
. Moreover, the results in Lemma
7.1
implies that the forms
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
are closed. Hence
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}
will also be closed. This concludes the proof.
∎

7.1
Proof of Theorem
1.3

Recall that we assumes
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}
is sesquilinear form which satisfies Assumption
1.2
with the numbers
(1,\mu)
with
\mu>0
. Then for the Friedrichs extension of
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}
we have

\big|\Tr[\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A(\hbar))]-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(a_{0}(x,p))\,dxdp\big|\leq C\hbar^{1-d},

Proof of Theorem
1.3
.

From Lemma
7.3
we get the existence of the operator
A(\hbar)
as a Friedrichs extension. Combing Proposition
2.5
and Lemma
7.1
we get the existence of two
\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible operators
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
of regularity
1
satisfying Assumption
4.1
for all
\varepsilon
sufficiently small such that

A_{\varepsilon}^{-}(\hbar)\leq A(\hbar)\leq A_{\varepsilon}^{+}(\hbar),

in the sense of quadratic forms. Moreover the operators
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
have the following expansions

A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)=\sum_{j=0}^{2m}\hbar^{j}\OpW(a^{\pm}_{\varepsilon,j}).

(7.18)

In particular we have that

a^{\pm}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)=\sum_{|\alpha|,|\beta|\leq m}a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)p^{\alpha+\beta}\pm C_{1}\varepsilon^{k+\mu}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}p^{2\alpha}

(7.19)

Moreover from Proposition
2.5
we also get that
0
is a non-critical value for the operators
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
.
What remains in order to be able to apply
Theorem
6.5
for the two operators
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
is the existence of a

\tilde{\nu}>0
such that the preimage of
(-\infty,\tilde{\nu}]

under
a^{\pm}_{\varepsilon,0}
is compact. By the
ellipticity of the operators we have that the preimage is compact in
p
. Hence if we
choose
\tilde{\nu}=\frac{\nu}{2}
and note that as in the proof of
Proposition
2.5
we have the estimate

|\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|,\left|\beta\right|\leq m}(a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)-a_{\alpha\beta}(x))p^{\alpha+\beta}\pm C_{1}\varepsilon^{1+\mu}(1+p^{2})^{m}|\leq C\varepsilon^{1+\mu},

(7.20)

since we can assume
p
to be in a compact set. This implies the
inclusion

\{(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\,|\,a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p)\leq\frac{\nu}{2}\}\subseteq\{(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\,|\,a_{0}(x,p)\leq\frac{\nu}{2}+C\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\}.

Hence for a sufficiently small
\varepsilon
we have that

\{(x,p)\in\mathbb{R}^{2d}\,|\,a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p)\leq\frac{\nu}{2}\}
is compact due to our assumptions. Now by
Theorem
6.5
we get for sufficiently small
\hbar

and
\varepsilon\geq\hbar^{1-\delta}
for a positive

\delta<1
that

|\Tr[\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\hbar))]-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))\,dxdp|\leq C\hbar^{1-d}.

(7.21)

Here we choose
\delta=\frac{\mu}{1+\mu}
. Now if we consider the
following difference between integrals

\displaystyle|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))\,dxdp-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(a_{0}(x,p))\,dxdp|

(7.22)

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{[-C\varepsilon^{1+\mu},C\varepsilon^{1+\mu}]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))\,dxdp\leq\tilde{C}\varepsilon^{1+\mu},

for
\varepsilon
and hence
\hbar
sufficiently small. Where we in
the last inequality have used the non-critical condition. By
combining (
7.21
) and (
7.22
) we get

|\Tr[\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\hbar))]-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))\,dxdp|\leq C\hbar^{1-d}+\tilde{C}\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\hbar^{-d}.

(7.23)

If we take
\varepsilon=\hbar^{1-\delta}
we have that

\varepsilon^{1+\mu}=\hbar^{(1+\mu)(1-\delta)}=\hbar.

Hence (
7.23
) with this choice of
\delta
and

\varepsilon
gives the estimate

|\Tr[\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A_{\varepsilon}^{\pm}(\hbar))]-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))\,dxdp|\leq C\hbar^{1-d}.

(7.24)

Now as the framing operators satisfied the relation

A_{\varepsilon}^{-}(\hbar)\leq A(\hbar)\leq A_{\varepsilon}^{+}(\hbar)

in the sense of quadratic forms we get by the min-max-theorem the relation

\Tr[\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A_{\varepsilon}^{+}(\hbar))]\leq\Tr[\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A(\hbar))]\leq\Tr[\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A_{\varepsilon}^{-}(\hbar))].

Combining this with (
7.24
) we get the estimate

|\Tr[\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(A(\hbar))]-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\infty,0]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p))\,dxdp|\leq C\hbar^{1-d}.

(7.25)

Which is the desired estimate and this ends the proof.
∎

7.2
Proof of Theorem
1.5

Recall that we a given a number
\gamma
in
(0,1]
and a sesquilinear form
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}
which satisfies Assumption
1.2
with the numbers
(2,\mu)
, where we suppose
\mu=0
if
\gamma<1
and
\mu>0
if
\gamma=1
. Then for the Friedrichs extension of
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}
we have

\big|\Tr[(A(\hbar))^{\gamma}_{-}]-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}+\hbar\gamma a_{1}(x,p)(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}_{-}\,dxdp\big|\leq C\hbar^{1+\gamma-d},

for all sufficiently small
\hbar
where the symbol
a_{1}(x,p)
is defined as

a_{1}(x,p)=i\sum_{|\alpha|,|\beta|\leq m}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\beta_{j}-\alpha_{j}}{2}\partial_{x_{j}}a_{\alpha\beta}(x)p^{\alpha+\beta-\eta_{j}},

(7.26)

where
\eta_{j}
is the multi index with all entries zero except the j’th which is one.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

The start of the proof is analogous to the proof of Theorem
1.3
. Again from Lemma
7.3
we get the existence of the operator
A(\hbar)
and by combing Proposition
2.5
and Lemma
7.1
we get the existence of two
\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible operators
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
of regularity
2
satisfying Assumption
4.1
for all
\varepsilon
sufficiently small such that

A_{\varepsilon}^{-}(\hbar)\leq A(\hbar)\leq A_{\varepsilon}^{+}(\hbar),

in the sense of quadratic forms. Moreover the operators
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
have the following expansions

A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)=\sum_{j=0}^{2m}\hbar^{j}\OpW(a^{\pm}_{\varepsilon,j}).

(7.27)

In particular we have that

a^{\pm}_{\varepsilon,0}(x,p)=\sum_{|\alpha|,|\beta|\leq m}a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)p^{\alpha+\beta}\pm C_{1}\varepsilon^{2+\mu}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq m}p^{2\alpha}

(7.28)

and

a^{\pm}_{\varepsilon,1}(x,p)=i\sum_{|\alpha|,|\beta|\leq m}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\beta_{j}-\alpha_{j}}{2}\partial_{x_{j}}a_{\alpha\beta}^{\varepsilon}(x)p^{\alpha+\beta-\eta_{j}}.

(7.29)

Moreover from Proposition
2.5
we also get that
0
is a non-critical value for the operators
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. Just as before we also get that there exist
\tilde{\nu}
such that
a_{\varepsilon,0}^{-1}((-\infty,\tilde{\nu}])
is compact for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
. Hence we are in a situation where we can apply Theorem
6.6
for the operators
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
. This gives us that

\big|\Tr[(A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{\gamma}_{-}]-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}+\hbar\gamma a_{\varepsilon,1}^{\pm}(x,p)(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}_{-}\,dxdp\big|\leq C\hbar^{1+\gamma-d}.

(7.30)

What remains is to compare the phase space integrals. We let
\kappa>0
be a number such that
a_{0}(x,p)
is non-critical on
a_{0}^{-1}(-2\kappa,2\kappa)
. We then do the following splitting of integrals

\displaystyle\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}\,dxdx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}\,dxdp\big|

(7.31)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-c,-\kappa]}(a_{0}(x,p))\big[(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}-(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}\big]\,dxdp\big|

\displaystyle+\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\kappa,0]}(a_{0}(x,p))\big[(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}-(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}\big]\,dxdp\big|,

where
c>0
is a number such that
a_{0}(x,p)-1\geq-c
for all
(x,p)
. On the preimage
a_{0}^{-1}((-\infty,-\kappa])
we have that
a_{0,\varepsilon}^{\pm}
will be bounded from above by
-\frac{\kappa}{2}
for all
\varepsilon
sufficiently small. Hence using that the map
t\mapsto(t)^{\gamma}_{-}
is globally Lipschitz continuous when restricted to any interval of the form
(-\infty,-\frac{\kappa}{2})
we get that

\displaystyle\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-c,-\kappa]}(a_{0}(x,p))\big[(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}-(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}\big]\,dxdp\big|

(7.32)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-c,-\kappa]}(a_{0}(x,p))|a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p)-a_{0}(x,p)|\,dxdp\leq C\varepsilon^{2+\mu},

where we in the last inequality have used estimates similar to the ones used in the proof of Proposition
2.5
. Before we estimate the second term in (
7.31
) we note that for all
\gamma
in
(0,1]
and
t\leq 0
we have that

(t)_{-}^{\gamma}=\gamma\int_{t}^{0}(-s)^{\gamma-1}\,ds.

Using this observation we get that

\displaystyle\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\kappa,0]}(a_{0}(x,p))\big[(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}-(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}\big]\,dxdp\big|

(7.33)

\displaystyle=\big|\gamma\int_{-\frac{3\kappa}{2}}^{0}(-s)^{\gamma-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\kappa,0]}(a_{0}(x,p))\big[\bm{1}_{(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p),0]}(s)-\bm{1}_{(a_{0}(x,p),0]}(s)\big]\,dxdpds\big|

\displaystyle\leq\gamma\int_{-\frac{3\kappa}{2}}^{0}(-s)^{\gamma-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}\bm{1}_{(-\kappa,0]}(a_{0}(x,p))\bm{1}_{[s-\tilde{c}\varepsilon^{2+\mu},s+\tilde{c}\varepsilon^{2+\mu}]}(a_{0}(x,p))\,dxdpds\leq C\varepsilon^{2+\mu}

where we in the last inequality have used the non-critical assumption. Combining (
7.31
), (
7.32
) and (
7.33
) we get

\displaystyle\big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}\,dxdx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}\,dxdp\big|\leq C\varepsilon^{2+\mu}.

(7.34)

Next we consider the difference

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{\varepsilon,1}^{\pm}(x,p)(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}_{-}\,dxdp-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{1}(x,p)(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}_{-}\,dxdp\Big|

(7.35)

Firstly we notice that from Proposition
2.5
we get that

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{\varepsilon,1}^{\pm}(x,p)(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}_{-}\,dxdp-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{1}(x,p)(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}_{-}\,dxdp\Big|\leq C\varepsilon^{1+\mu}.

(7.36)

Moreover, we have that

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{1}(x,p)(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}_{-}\,dxdp-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{1}(x,p)(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}_{-}\,dxdp\Big|

(7.37)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{1}(x,p)\big[\bm{1}_{(-\kappa,0]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))(-a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}-\bm{1}_{(-\kappa,0]}(a_{0}(x,p))(-a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}\big]\,dxdp\Big|

\displaystyle+C\varepsilon^{2+\mu},

where the number
\kappa>0
is the same as above. In this estimate we have used that

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{1}(x,p)\big[\bm{1}_{(-c,-\kappa]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))(-a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}-\bm{1}_{(-c,-\kappa]}(a_{0}(x,p))(-a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}\big]\,dxdp\Big|

(7.38)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2+\mu},

where the arguments are analogous to the ones used for estimating the first difference of phase space integrals after recalling that the map
t\rightarrow(t)^{\gamma-1}_{-}
is Lipschitz continuous when restricted to a bounded interval of the form
(-a,-b)
, where
0<b<a
.

On the pre-image
a_{0}^{-1}((-\kappa,0])
we have that

|\nabla_{p}a_{0}(x,p)|\geq c_{0}>0.

(7.39)

Then by using a partition of unity we may assume that
|\partial_{p_{1}}a_{0}(x,p)|\geq c>0
on
a_{0}^{-1}((-\kappa,0])
. From the construction of the approximating symbols it follows that for
\varepsilon
sufficiently small we get that
|\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p)|\geq\tilde{c}
when
a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p)\in(-\kappa,0]
.
As in the proof of Theorem
6.1
we now define the maps
F^{\pm}
and
F
by

F^{\pm}:(x,p)\rightarrow(x_{1},\dots,x_{d},a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p),p_{2},\dots,p_{d})

and the map
F
is defined similarly with
a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p)
replaced by
a_{0}(x,p)
. The determinants for the Jacobian matrices are given by

\det(DF^{\pm})=\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}\quad\text{and}\quad\det(DF)=\partial_{p_{1}}a_{0}.

This gives us that the inverse maps (
G^{\pm}
,
G
) exists and they will be close in the sense that

|G^{\pm}(y,q)-G(y,q)|\leq C\varepsilon^{2+\mu}

(7.40)

on the domain, where both functions are defined. To see this note that they will only be different in one coordinate and note that these coordinates will be determined by the equation
a_{0}(y,(p_{1},q_{2},\dots,q_{d}))=q_{1}
and similarly for the two other functions. With a change of variables we get that

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{1}(x,p)\big[\bm{1}_{(-\kappa,0]}(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))(-a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}-\bm{1}_{(-\kappa,0]}(a_{0}(x,p))(-a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}\big]\,dxdp\Big|

(7.41)

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\Big|\int_{\Omega}\frac{a_{1}(G^{\pm}(x,p))\bm{1}_{(-\kappa,0]}(p_{1})(-p_{1})^{\gamma-1}}{\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(G^{\pm}(x,p))}-\frac{a_{1}(G(x,p))\bm{1}_{(-\kappa,0]}(p_{1})(-p_{1})^{\gamma-1}}{\partial_{p_{1}}a_{0}(G(x,p))}\,dxdp\Big|

\displaystyle\leq{}

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\Big|\frac{a_{1}(G^{\pm}(x,p))}{\partial_{p_{1}}a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(G^{\pm}(x,p))}-\frac{a_{1}(G(x,p))}{\partial_{p_{1}}a_{0}(G(x,p))}\Big|\bm{1}_{(-\kappa,0]}(p_{1})(-p_{1})^{\gamma-1}\,dxdp\leq C\varepsilon^{1+\mu},

where
\Omega
is a compact subset of
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
. The last estimate follows similarly to the previous estimates. From combining (
7.35
), (
7.36
), (
7.37
) and (
7.41
) we get that

\Big|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{\varepsilon,1}^{\pm}(x,p)(a_{\varepsilon,0}^{\pm}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}_{-}\,dxdp-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}a_{1}(x,p)(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}_{-}\,dxdp\Big|\leq C\varepsilon^{1+\mu}

(7.42)

Now by choosing
\delta=1-\frac{1+\gamma}{2+\mu}
and combining (
7.30
), (
7.34
) (
7.42
) we obtain that

\displaystyle\big|\Tr[(A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar))^{\gamma}_{-}]-\frac{1}{(2\pi\hbar)^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2d}}(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma}_{-}+\hbar\gamma a_{1}(x,p)(a_{0}(x,p))^{\gamma-1}_{-}\,dxdp\big|

(7.43)

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\hbar^{1+\gamma-d}+C_{2}\hbar^{-d}\varepsilon^{2+\mu}+C_{3}\hbar^{1-d}\varepsilon^{1+\mu}\leq C\hbar^{1+\gamma-d}

Now as the framing operators satisfied the relation

A_{\varepsilon}^{-}(\hbar)\leq A(\hbar)\leq A_{\varepsilon}^{+}(\hbar)

in the sense of quadratic forms we get by the min-max-theorem the relation

\Tr[(A_{\varepsilon}^{+}(\hbar))^{\gamma}_{-}]\leq\Tr[(A(\hbar))^{\gamma}_{-}]\leq\Tr[(A_{\varepsilon}^{-}(\hbar))^{\gamma}_{-}].

These inequalities combined with (
7.43
) gives the desired estimates and this concludes the proof.
∎

7.3
Proof of Theorem
1.9
and Theorem
1.10

Recall that we assume
B(\hbar)
is an admissible operator and that
B(\hbar)
satisfies Assumption
1.8
with
\hbar
in
(0,\hbar_{0}]
. Moreover we assume that
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}
be a sesquilinear form which satisfies Assumption
1.2
with the numbers
(k,\mu)
, where the value of the two numbers
k
and
\mu
depend on which Theorem one consider. We defined the symbol
\tilde{b}_{0}(x,p)
to be

\tilde{b}_{0}(x,p)=b_{0}(x,p)+\sum_{\left|\alpha\right|,\left|\beta\right|\leq m}a_{\alpha\beta}(x)p^{\alpha+\beta}.

(7.44)

Finally we supposed that there is a
\nu
such that
\tilde{b}_{0}^{-1}((-\infty,\nu])
is compact and there is
c>0
such that

|\nabla_{p}\tilde{b}_{0}(x,p)|\geq c\quad\text{for all }(x,p)\in\tilde{b}_{0}^{-1}(\{0\}).

(7.45)

Proof of Theorem
1.9
and Theorem
1.10
.

From Lemma
7.3
we get the existence of the self-adjointoperator
A(\hbar)
as the Friedrichs extension
\mathcal{A}_{\hbar}
. Since the Schwartz functions are in the domain of the operators
A(\hbar)
and
B(\hbar)
we can define their form sum
\tilde{B}(\hbar)=B(\hbar)+A(\hbar)
as a selfadjoint, lower semi-bounded operator see e.g.
[
21
, Proposition 10.22]
. Using Proposition
2.5
and by arguing as in Lemma
7.1
we get the existence of two
\hbar
-
\varepsilon
-admissible operators
\tilde{B}^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
of regularity
1
or
2
satisfying Assumption
4.1
for all
\varepsilon
sufficiently small such that

\tilde{B}_{\varepsilon}^{-}(\hbar)\leq\tilde{B}(\hbar)\leq\tilde{B}_{\varepsilon}^{+}(\hbar).

In the sense of quadratic forms. In fact we will have that
\tilde{B}^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)=B(\hbar)+A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
, where
A^{\pm}_{\varepsilon}(\hbar)
is the operator from Lemma
7.1
. From here the proof is either analogous to that of Theorem
1.3
if it is the proof of Theorem
1.9
. If it is the proof of Theorem
1.10
it will be analogous to the proof of Theorem
1.5
from here.
∎

Appendix A
Faà di Bruno formula

In this appendix we will recall some results about multivariate
differentiation. In particular we will recall the Faà di Bruno formula, which gives a multivariate chain rule for any number of derivatives:

Theorem A.1

(Faà di Bruno formula)
.

Let
f
be a function from
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
g
a function from

C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Then for all multi indices
\alpha
with

\left|\alpha\right|\geq 1
the following formula holds:

\partial_{x}^{\alpha}f(g(x))=\sum_{k=1}^{\left|\alpha\right|}f^{(k)}(g(x))\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{k}=\alpha\\
\left|\alpha_{j}\right|>0\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}g(x)\cdots\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{k}}g(x),

where
f^{(k)}
is the
k
’th derivative of f. The second sum should
be understod as a sum over all ways to split the multi index

\alpha
in
k
non-trivial parts. The numbers

c_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k}}
’s are combinatorial constants
independent of the functions.

A proof of the Faà di Bruno formula can be found in

[
4
]
, where they prove the formula in greater generality
then stated here. It is also possible to find the constants from their
proof, but for our purpose here the exact values of the constants are
not important. The next Corollary is the Faà di Bruno formula in the
case of
\mathbb{R}^{2d}
instead of just
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. But we need to control
the exact number of derivatives in the first
d
components hence it
is stated separately.

Corollary A.2
.

Let
f
be a function from
C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
a(x,p)
a function from

C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{x}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}_{p})
. Then for all multi indices
\alpha

and
\beta
with
\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|\geq 1
the following
formula holds:

\partial_{p}^{\beta}\partial_{x}^{\alpha}f(a(x,p))=\sum_{k=1}^{\left|\alpha\right|+\left|\beta\right|}f^{(k)}(a(x,p))\sum_{\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha,\beta)}c_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k}}^{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}}\partial_{p}^{\beta_{1}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}a(x,p)\cdots\partial_{p}^{\beta_{k}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{k}}a(x,p),

where the set
\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha,\beta)
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha,\beta)=\{(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k},

\displaystyle\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{2kd}\

\displaystyle|\ \sum_{l=1}^{k}\alpha_{l}=\alpha,\,\sum_{l=1}^{k}\beta_{l}=\beta,\,\max(\left|\alpha_{l}\right|,\left|\beta_{l}\right|)\geq 1\,\forall l\}.

The second sum is a sum over all elements in the set

\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha,\beta)
, the constants

c_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k}}^{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{k}}
are
combinatorial constants independent of the functions and
f^{(k)}

is the
k
’th derivative of the function
f
.

We will only give a short sketch of the proof of this corollary.

Sketch.

We have by the Faà di Bruno formula (Theoram
A.1
) the identity

\partial_{x}^{\alpha}f(a(x,p))=\sum_{k=1}^{\left|\alpha\right|}f^{(k)}(a(x,p))\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{k}=\alpha\\
\left|\alpha_{j}\right|>0\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k}}\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}a(x,p)\cdots\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{k}}a(x,p)

(A.1)

By Leibniz’s formula we have

\displaystyle\partial_{p}^{\beta}

\displaystyle\partial_{x}^{\alpha}f(a(x,p))

(A.2)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma\leq\beta}\binom{\beta}{\gamma}\sum_{k=1}^{\left|\alpha\right|}\partial_{p}^{\gamma}f^{(k)}(a(x,p))\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\alpha_{1}+\cdots+\alpha_{k}=\alpha\\
\left|\alpha_{j}\right|>0\end{subarray}}c_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{k}}\partial_{p}^{\beta-\gamma}[\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}a(x,p)\cdots\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{k}}a(x,p)].

In order to obtain the form stated in the corollary we need to use the Faà di Bruno formula on the terms

\partial_{p}^{\gamma}f^{(k)}(a(x,p)),

(A.3)

and we need to use Leibniz’s formula (multiple times) on the terms

\partial_{p}^{\beta-\gamma}[\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{1}}a(x,p)\cdots\partial_{x}^{\alpha_{k}}a(x,p)].

(A.4)

If this is done, then by using some algebra the stated form can be obtained. The particular form of the index set
\mathcal{I}_{k}(\alpha,\beta)
also follows from this algebra.
∎
